17 
EARNINGS ADJUSTMENT MECHANISMS 
18 
Q. 
Provide a brief overview of EAMs. 
19 
A. 
EAMs are metrics with targets and associated 
20 
earnings opportunities designed to align utility 
21 
shareholder interests with New York State energy 
22 
policy goals.  If the utility meets the EAM 
23 
targets, benefits are achieved, and utility 
24 
543


--- Page 21 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
4 
 
shareholders earn financial rewards.  The 
1 
Commission initially approved EAMs in the May 
2 
19, 2016 Order Adopting a Ratemaking and Utility 
3 
Revenue Model Policy Framework in Case 14-M-
4 
0101, referred to as the Track Two Order. 
5 
Q. 
Provide a brief overview of the EAM requirements 
6 
set forth in the Track Two Order. 
7 
A. 
Pages 59-60 of the Track Two Order explained the 
8 
Commission’s expectation that EAMs would play a 
9 
transitional role until other forms of market-
10 
based revenues are available.  The Track Two 
11 
Order established the Commission’s preference 
12 
for: outcome-based EAMs, see pages 61-65; EAMs 
13 
that are generally positive-only, see pages 66-
14 
67; a maximum amount of earnings totaling 100 
15 
basis points, see page 68; and establishing EAM 
16 
awards on a sliding scale with the size of 
17 
awards growing with greater achievement, see 
18 
page 77.  The Track Two Order also set the 
19 
Commission’s expectations for the types of EAM 
20 
metrics it would consider and stated that rate 
21 
proceedings are the proper venue for developing 
22 
EAMs. 
23 
Q. 
What EAM metrics were discussed in the Track Two 
24 
544


--- Page 22 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
5 
 
Order? 
1 
A. 
In general, the Track Two Order discussed EAMs 
2 
designed predominantly for electric utilities 
3 
only.  The Track Two Order implemented a System 
4 
Efficiency EAM based on both peak load reduction 
5 
and improvements in load factor, see pages 73-
6 
77; an Energy Efficiency EAM based on achieving 
7 
greater energy efficiency program savings or 
8 
cost savings and electric usage intensity across 
9 
the utility’s territory, see pages 82-83; and an 
10 
Interconnection EAM based on an evaluation of 
11 
application quality and satisfaction of 
12 
applicants with the process, see pages 86-87.  
13 
Although the Track Two Order states, on page 89, 
14 
that no general EAM is necessary for Customer 
15 
Engagement and Information Access, it did invite 
16 
proposals for such an EAM based on customer 
17 
uptake for opt-in innovative rates and 
18 
initiatives related to electric vehicle adoption 
19 
and ground source heat pumps, as explained on 
20 
pages 89-90.  Similarly, on pages 90-91, the 
21 
Track Two Order invited proposals related to 
22 
incentivizing greenhouse gas reductions. 
23

Q. 
Please describe the first statewide policy 
18 
proceeding in which the Commission has further 
19 
refined its position on EAMs. 
20 
A. 
On March 15, 2018 in case 15-M-0252, the 
21 
Commission issued its Order Authorizing Utility-
22 
Administered Energy Efficiency Portfolio Budgets 
23 
and Targets for 2019–2020 (March 2018 Energy 
24 
547


--- Page 25 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
8 
 
Efficiency Order). On pages 20-27 of this Order, 
1 
the Commission required all programmatic EAMs 
2 
proposed after March 15, 2018 to be calculated 
3 
and awarded based on verified gross energy 
4 
savings.  On page 28 o the order, the Commission 
5 
also required that the utilities file an 
6 
Evaluation Measurement & Verification (EM&V) 
7 
plan demonstrating when and how EM&V activities 
8 
will be performed to support awarding EAM 
9 
financial incentives based reported energy 
10 
efficiency savings. 
11 
Q. 
Please define gross savings. 
12 
A. 
Gross savings are energy savings that result 
13 
directly from program-related actions taken by 
14 
participants in an energy efficiency program, 
15 
regardless of why they participated.  This topic 
16 
is also discussed in the direct testimony of the 
17 
Staff Efficiency and Sustainability Panel. 
18 
Q. 
Please define verified gross savings. 
19 
A. 
Verified gross savings are the values reported 
20 
by an independent evaluator after the energy 
21 
efficiency program activities and gross savings 
22 
analysis are complete.  Verified gross savings 
23 
are distinct from gross savings in two ways.  
24 
548


--- Page 26 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
9 
 
First, verified gross savings are the product of 
1 
a complete gross savings analysis using methods 
2 
consistent with industry standard best 
3 
practices.  Second, verified gross savings are 
4 
produced by an independent evaluator, not by the 
5 
utility. 
6 
Q. 
Please define the process used to determine 
7 
verified gross savings. 
8 
A. 
As part of the energy efficiency budgets, each 
9 
utility has also been provided a budget for EM&V 
10 
activities and studies.  EM&V is a broadly 
11 
inclusive description of any activities intended 
12 
to determine energy efficiency program impacts, 
13 
including quantifying the savings realized by 
14 
the program.  The ratio of the savings 
15 
determined after verification to the gross 
16 
savings is called the verified gross savings 
17 
realization rate. 
18 
Q. 
Please describe the second statewide policy 
19 
proceeding in which the Commission has further 
20 
refined its position on EAMs since the Track Two 
21 
Order? 
22 
A. 
On December 13, 2018 in case 18-M-0084, the 
23 
Commission issued the Order Adopting Accelerated 
24 
549


--- Page 27 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
10 
 
Energy Efficiency Targets (December 2018 Energy 
1 
Efficiency Order).  In this Order, the 
2 
Commission established a new paradigm in Energy 
3 
Efficiency EAMs by requiring utilities to 
4 
develop and propose new “share the savings” EAMs

related to their energy efficiency programs.  On

8 
energy efficiency programs will continue to be

achieved below authorized budgets or by

for the same budget.  To encourage longer lived

19

20

21 
efficiency EAMs to use a dollars-per-lifetime

MMBTU basis.  On pages 67 and 68 of the Order, 
23 
the Commission expressed its preference for 
24 
550


--- Page 28 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
11 
 
“share the savings” energy efficiency EAMs, as

they measure near term results commensurate with

the length of three-year rate plans.  As a

Q. 
Why should the Commission adopt more aggressive 
2 
EAM achievement levels? 
3 
A. 
The Companies’ target-setting methodology 
4 
results in achievement levels that do not 
5

6

7

A. 
Third, we propose that the EAMs ultimately be 
17 
expressed in terms of absolute dollars instead 
18 
of basis points.  While we express our potential 
19 
earnings in basis points in our testimony, we 
20

21

22

23 
Q. 
Explain why the Panel recommends that EAMs be 
24 
555


--- Page 33 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
16 
 
awarded in terms of dollars and not in basis 
1 
points. 
2 
A. 
Since the value of a basis point fluctuates over 
3 
time, as it is directly related to the amount of 
4 
plant in service, basing EAMs strictly on basis 
5 
points could create a perverse incentive for the 
6 
Companies to increase its net plant-in-service 
7 
to inflate the value of a basis point.  Instead, 
8 
the basis points assigned to each EAM should be 
9 
converted into absolute dollar figures based on 
10 
the equity ratio and net plant-in-service 
11 
targets ultimately approved by the Commission in 
12 
these proceedings. 
13 
Q. 
What is the Panel’s fourth overall 
14 
recommendation? 
15 
A. 
Fourth, in the event that a settlement is 
16 
reached resulting in a multi-year rate plan, we 
17 
recommend that the EAM metrics, targets, and 
18 
financial incentive levels be set for the full 
19 
term of the rate plan. 
20 
Q. 
Why should the EAM metrics, targets, and 
21 
financial incentive levels be set for the full 
22 
term of a multi-year rate plan? 
23 
A. 
A term of a multi-year rate plan strikes a 
24 
556


--- Page 34 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
17 
 
reasonable balance between providing a long-term 
1 
incentive structure around which the Company 
2 
will be able to plan its business and 
3 
operations, and ensuring that the metrics, 
4 
targets, and financial incentives remain 
5 
reasonable and relevant.  Furthermore, setting 
6 
the EAM metric targets for multiple years helps 
7 
to reduce a utility gaming opportunity whereby 
8 
the Companies might have an incentive, if EAMs 
9 
are reset each year based on performance during 
10 
the prior year, to artificially depress its 
11 
progress toward the metric targets if it can 
12 
reasonably expect that metric targets would be 
13 
reduced in a subsequent proceeding, thus 
14 
requiring less effort for greater financial 
15 
reward. 
16 
Q. 
Are there risks involved in setting multi-year 
17 
EAM metrics, targets, and financial incentives? 
18 
A. 
Setting EAM metric targets for a multi-year term 
19 
does present a risk that targets may be set 
20 
either too strict, such that the utility is not 
21 
able to achieve the targets regardless of its 
22 
actions, or too lenient, such that the utility 
23 
earns an incentive with very little effort.  
24 
557


--- Page 35 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
18 
 
However, it is our opinion that this risk is 
1 
more than balanced given our process of 
2 
developing targets that reflect achievable goals 
3 
through utility intervention and efforts and the 
4 
benefits of providing a stable and predictable 
5 
incentive structure for KEDLI and KEDNY.  This 
6 
combination is beneficial for the Companies, 
7 
their customers, and the State of New York. 
8

Q. 
Explain how the Incremental Energy Efficiency 
10

11

12 
A. 
There are three pathways toward earning a 
13 
financial award under the Share the Savings 
14 
metric: (1) reducing total energy efficiency 
15 
portfolio expenditures; (2) increasing the first 
16 
year MMBTU savings achieved; and (3) increasing 
17 
the average effective useful lifetime (EUL) of 
18 
the Companies’ portfolios by focusing on longer-
19 
lived measures.  Pursuing any or all of these 
20 
three pathways can result in a significant 
21 
reduction to total cost per lifetime MMBTU.  To 
22 
achieve the Annual MMBTU EAM, however, the 
23 
Companies would have to increase the first year 
24 
573


--- Page 51 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
34
MMBTU savings achieved by the portfolio, which 
1 
the Companies will already be incentivized to do 
2

3 
Q.
Please explain your recommended Share the 
4 
Savings metric. 
5 
A.
We recommend a three-step process to determine 
6 
the financial incentives under the Share the 
7 
Savings EAM.  First, the Companies would 
8 
calculate the actual dollar per lifetime MMBTU 
9 
program run-rate it achieves each year.  To do 
10 
this, each Company would divide the cost of its 
11 
total energy efficiency portfolio by the product 
12 
of the portfolio’s first-year MMBTU savings and 
13 
the EUL of the portfolio.  Second, each Company 
14 
would then subtract the annual lifetime run rate 
15 
from a baseline annual lifetime run rate.  The 
16 
baseline run rate would be calculated by 
17 
dividing the Commission-authorized portfolio 
18 
budget by the product of the Commission-
19 
authorized portfolio savings target and the 
20 
Company’s portfolio EUL.  Third, each Company 
21 
would multiply the difference in dollars per 
22 
lifetime MMBTU calculated in the second step by 
23 
the actual annual lifetime MMBTU achieved by 
24 
574


--- Page 52 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
35 
 
that Company’s portfolio, multiplied by that 
1 
Company’s share of savings percentage, 30 
2 
percent.  We provide an illustrative example of 
3 
this process in Exhibit___(SEAMP-3). 
4 
Q. 
How did the Panel develop its proposed 30 
5 
percent Company-share of savings? 
6 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit__(SEAMP-3) we benchmarked 
7 
the recent Commission-approved electric and gas 
8 
energy efficiency EAM targets for other 
9 
utilities.  Based on this exercise, we 
10 
determined that a roughly 50% improvement in 
11 
energy efficiency program run rates were needed 
12 
to achieve the maximum EAM award.  In each of 
13 
the utilities benchmarked, the Commission has 
14 
awarded a total 10 basis points to programmatic 
15 
gas energy efficiency EAMs.  As shown in 
16 
Exhibit__(SEAMP-3), we then calculated the 
17 
resulting Company-share of savings percentage 
18 
for approximately 10 basis points of incentives 
19 
based on a 50 percent reduction in energy 
20 
efficiency portfolio costs per lifetime MMBTU.  
21 
Based on these calculations we determined that a 
22 
30 percent Company-share of the savings is 
23 
appropriate.

Q.

24

proposed are inappropriately low. 
1 
A. 
The amount of variation in recent historical 
2

4 
4), the average annual monthly low income 
5

6

7

8

9 
percent decrease in year-over-year annual 
10 
average monthly low income customer bills, which 
11 
would provide the Companies with a 10 basis 
12 
point financial reward, represents just 0.14 
13 
standard deviations. 
14 
Q. 
What are standard deviations? 
15 
A. 
A standard deviation is a statistic that 
16 
describes how much variation there is in a set 
17 
of data.  The greater the amount of variation in 
18 
a data set, the greater the standard deviation.  
19 
The standard deviation of a data set can also 
20 
tell us how likely a particular outcome is to be 
21 
the result of random, naturally occurring, 
22 
variation in a given data set.  The greater 
23 
number of standard deviations a particular 
24 
583


--- Page 61 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
44 
 
outcome is away from the average of the data 
1 
set, the greater likelihood that that outcome 
2 
was not the result of random variation and was 
3 
instead influenced by some outside factor, for 
4 
example, utility efforts to reduce low income 
5 
customer bills. 
6 
Q. 
At 0.14 standard deviations, how likely is it 
7 
that reductions in low income customer bills are 
8 
the result of increased utility efforts and not 
9 
random chance? 
10 
A. 
At 0.14 standard deviations, we can only be 
11 
about 10 percent certain that the variation from 
12 
the average is a result of Company intervention 
13 
and not simple random variation. 
14 
Q. 
Are there examples from other utilities’ EAMs 
15 
where the standard deviation or other similar 
16 
descriptive statistic have been the basis for 
17 
setting EAM targets? 
18 
A. 
Yes.  The standard errors of regression, a 
19 
descriptive statistic similar to standard 
20 
deviations – essentially the standard deviations 
21 
of a set of data from a linear regression – has 
22 
been used as the basis for the Electric Energy 
23 
Intensity metrics at Niagara Mohawk, Central 
24 
584

Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP

17

18

geothermal systems would otherwise use to 
20 
determine the amount of natural gas offset by 
21

23 
multiply the amount of natural gas offset by 
24 
588


--- Page 66 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP

geothermal customers by the lifecycle carbon 
1

3 
from geothermal customers offsetting natural gas 
4 
usage. 
5 
Q. 
How do the Companies propose to measure carbon 
6

7

13 
CO2 emissions rates on a metric ton of CO2

15 
gas and RNG.

PLATFORM SERVICE REVENUES 
9 
Q. 
What are Platform Service Revenues (PSRs)? 
10 
A. 
The Track Two Order defines PSRs as a new form 
11 
of utility revenues associated with the 
12 
operation or facilitation of distribution-level 
13 
markets.  The Track Two Order also notes that 
14 
PSRs are expected to take the place of EAMs in 
15 
the long term as the utilities transition from 
16 
their traditional role as distribution utilities 
17 
to their new role as operators of the 
18 
distributed system platform. 
19 
Q. 
Do KEDNY and KEDLI propose any PSRs in this rate 
20 
filing? 
21 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies propose two PSRs on page 116 
22 
of the FOH Panel’s direct testimony, one related 
23 
to their Energy Efficiency E-Commerce Platform, 
24 
594


--- Page 72 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
55 
 
and another related to their Utility Energy 
1 
Services Contracts (UESC) program.  For each of 
2 
these PSRs, the Companies propose to retain a 
3 
portion of the fees collected from program 
4 
vendors and participants and pass the remainder 
5 
back to customers. 
6 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ proposed PSR 
7 
related to the E-Commerce Platform. 
8 
A. 
On page 117 of the FOH Panel’s direct testimony, 
9 
the Companies propose to retain 20 percent of 
10 
the fees they collect from vendors participating 
11 
in their respective E-Commerce Platforms.  The 
12 
Companies propose to defer the remaining 80 
13 
percent of fees from the E-Commerce Platform for 
14 
future credit to customers. 
15 
Q. 
Did the Companies explain why they proposed to 
16 
retain a 20 percent share? 
17 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies explain on page 117 of the 
18 
FOH Panel’s direct testimony that they developed 
19 
their proposed E-Commerce Platform EAM to be 
20 
consistent with a similar PSR approved by the 
21 
Commission as part of the Rate Order issued on 
22 
March 15, 2018 in Cases 17-E-0238 and 17-G-0239 
23 
regarding Niagara Mohawk. 
24 
595


--- Page 73 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
56 
 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the Companies’ 
1 
proposed E-Commerce Platform PSR? 
2 
A. 
No.  The 20 percent share PSR approved by the 
3 
Commission as part of Cases 17-E-0238 and 17-G-
4 
0239 for Niagara Mohawk references electric 
5 
businesses only. 
6 
Q. 
Did the Track Two Order establish guidelines for 
7 
consideration of PSRs? 
8 
A. 
Yes, the Track Two Order establishes five 
9 
criteria for consideration of PSRs: (1) whether 
10 
the service facilitates the growth of markets; 
11 
(2) whether there is already a third-party 
12 
market for the service that adequately serves 
13 
all sectors of the market; (3) whether utility 
14 
economies of scale and/or existing utility 
15 
expertise are likely to result in a cost-
16 
effective stimulation of the market; (4) whether 
17 
utility provision of the service is likely to 
18 
prevent other providers from entering the 
19 
market; and (5) the extent to which a utility 
20 
has proposed placing shareholder funds at risk. 
21 
Q. 
Did the Companies’ demonstrate that the criteria 
22 
established in the Track Two Order were 
23 
satisfied? 
24 
596


--- Page 74 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
57 
 
A. 
We do not believe the Companies provide 
1 
substantive/reasonable distribution-level market 
2 
information to justify consideration of the PSR 
3 
in either their direct testimony or in response 
4 
to IR DPS-363. 
5 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ proposed PSR 
6 
related to the UESC Program. 
7 
A. 
On page 117 of the FOH Panel’s direct testimony, 
8 
the Companies propose to retain one-third of the 
9 
fees collected from UESC participants.  The 
10 
Companies propose to defer the remaining two-
11 
thirds of such fees for future credit to 
12 
customers.

Q. 
Did the Companies explain why they proposed to 
14 
retain a one-third share of the UESC Program 
15 
fees? 
16

Yes.  On pages 117 and 118 of the FOH Panel’s 
17

18

19

20

597


--- Page 75 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
58 
 
program.  The Companies argue that a higher 
1 
Company-share of revenues is warranted because

Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the Companies’ 
6 
proposed PSR related to the UESC program? 
7 
A. 
Partially.  We agree that the Companies should 
8 
be provided a PSR to encourage them to maximize 
9

10 
However, the Companies’ proposal to retain one-
11

12

Q. 
Why do you disagree with the Companies’ proposed 
14 
retention of one-third of UESC-related fees? 
15 
A. 
The Track Two Order established 20 percent as a 
16 
reasonable utility-share of revenues for PSRs 
17 
built upon programs without any shareholder 
18 
funds at risk, and stated that higher sharing 
19 
percentages might be reasonable depending on the 
20 
amount of shareholder funds at risk. 
21 
Q. 
Are any shareholder funds at risk related to the 
22 
UESC program? 
23 
A. 
No.  Regardless of whether or not the Companies 
24 
598


--- Page 76 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
59 
 
are able to convince customers to participate in 
1 
the UESC program, the Companies are made whole 
2 
for the costs of the program.  No shareholder 
3 
funds are at risk, as acknowledged by the 
4 
Companies in response to IR DPS-363, and 
5 
therefore the Companies should not receive a PSR 
6 
greater than 20 percent of revenues from the 
7 
UESC program. 
8 
Q.

9 
for a PSR related to the UESC program? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  Instead of the Companies’ proposed PSR 
11 
based on a one-third share of all UESC program 
12 
fees, we recommend that the Companies be able to 
13 
retain a PSR equal to 20 percent of the net 
14

15 
participants.

17 
A. 
The costs of the UESC program are recovered 
18 
through base rates.  Under our recommendation, 
19 
the Companies would apply 100 percent of the 
20 
fees collected from UESC program participants 
21 
toward offsetting the costs of the UESC program 
22 
until such costs are fully offset.  Once the 
23 
costs of the UESC program are offset, the 
24 
599


--- Page 77 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
60 
 
Companies would be eligible to retain 20 percent 
1 
of any further UESC program fees, with the 
2 
remainder deferred for customer benefit.  An 
3 
example of our recommended PSR is provided in 
4 
Exhibit___(SEAMP-5). 
5 
Q. 
Are there benefits to adopting your recommended 
6 
UESC-related PSR instead of the Companies’ 
7 
proposal? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  As shown in Attachment 2 to the Companies’ 
9 
response to IR DPS-362, under the Companies’ 
10 
proposed PSR, the Companies would receive 
11 
approximately double the earnings that accrue to 
12 
customers.  Further, as shown in 
13 
Exhibit___(SEAMP-5), unlike the Companies’ 
14 
proposal, our recommended PSR only rewards 
15 
success in the UESC program when it produces 
16 
fees above and beyond its costs.  Under this 
17 
paradigm, the Companies will have a significant 
18 
incentive for the UESC program to pay for itself 
19 
in a given year, and will thereafter have the 
20 
opportunity to retain a portion of those fees 
21 
once the program is profitable.

23

KEDLI EMPOWER REPLACEMENT INCENTIVE 
1 
Q.

2

4

11 
Under KEDLI’s existing EmPower Replacement 
12 
Program incentive mechanism, the Company earns 
13

16

A. 
Under the current mechanism, the Company earns a 
24 
601


--- Page 79 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

62 
 
reward of 0.007 percent of the program budget

for each household participating in Tier 4.

Historically, KEDLI has earned approximately 
5 
$122.88 per household participating in Tier 3,

and $245.76 per household participating in 
7 
Tier 4.  Since Tier 4 participants also 
8

$386.64 per household participating in both

Tier 3 and Tier 4.

Program, however, we recommend redesigning the

20

A. 
Currently KEDLI’s incentive per household will

24

per household, regardless of the program budget.  
22 
In addition, we recommend reducing the incentive 
23 
related to participation in Tier 4 of the 
24 
603

Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

64 
 
program.

4 
significantly outpaced the incentives related to

achieved under Tier 3.

to the main purpose of the EmPower Replacement

Program itself.

NON-PIPES ALTERNATIVES INCENTIVE MECHANISM 
3 
Q. 
Please provide a summary of the Companies’ 
4 
proposals regarding Non-Pipes Alternatives 
5 
(NPAs). 
6 
A. 
The FOH Panel briefly discusses its NPA-related 
7 
proposals on page 118 of its direct testimony, 
8 
with significant additional detail contained in 
9 
Exhibit___(FOH-11).  In Exhibit___(FOH-11), the 
10 
Companies propose: (1) a definition of NPAs; (2) 
11 
screening criteria for traditional 
12 
infrastructure projects to be considered for 
13 
development as an NPA; (3) eligibility 
14 
requirements for resources to participate in an 
15 
NPA project; (4) a shareholder incentive 
16 
mechanism; (5) NPA project cost and incentive 
17 
cost recovery mechanisms; and (6) associated 
18 
reporting requirements. 
19 
Q. 
How do the Companies define an NPA project? 
20 
A. 
The Companies define an NPA project as a 
21 
portfolio of resources used to delay, reduce, or 
22 
eliminate the need for traditional capital 
23 
investments to reinforce or expand gas supply 
24 
606


--- Page 84 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
67 
 
and distribution infrastructure, and which 
1 
reduces net utility plant in service.  The 
2 
Companies note that while their proposed 
3 
incentive mechanism would be limited to projects 
4 
that reduce utility net plant in service, they 
5 
would not be precluded from pursuing 
6 
opportunities to develop solutions for other 
7 
types of projects that would not reduce utility 
8 
net plant in service, but would produce societal 
9 
benefits. 
10 
Q. 
What types of projects would be included under 
11 
the Companies’ proposed incentive mechanism? 
12 
A. 
The Companies’ proposed incentive mechanism 
13 
would include any project that displaces KEDNY 
14 
or KEDLI capital expenditures, and therefore net 
15 
plant in service.  For example, the mechanism 
16 
would apply to a project that would eliminate 
17 
the need to replace gas distribution main in a 
18 
particular area. 
19 
Q. 
What types of projects would not be included 
20 
under the Companies’ proposed incentive 
21 
mechanism? 
22 
A. 
The incentive mechanism would not cover projects 
23 
that reduce the need for interstate pipelines, 
24 
607


--- Page 85 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
68 
 
since reductions to interstate pipeline capital 
1 
costs do not result in a reduction to 
2 
distribution utility net plant in service.  To 
3 
be clear, the Companies would still have the 
4 
opportunity to pursue such projects, however, 
5 
the explicit cost recovery and incentive 
6 
mechanisms proposed for NPAs in these 
7 
proceedings would not apply to those projects.  
8 
Instead, the Companies would have to separately 
9 
request authorization and cost recovery for such 
10 
projects. 
11 
Q. 
Please define “NPA screening criteria.” 
12 
A. 
NPA screening criteria are a set of requirements 
13 
which obligate the Companies to attempt to 
14 
develop an NPA project when all of the criteria 
15 
are met.  When considering a capital project, 
16 
the Companies must compare the project against 
17 
the screening criteria.  If the project meets 
18 
all of the screening criteria, the Companies 
19 
must attempt to develop an NPA project to 
20 
implement in lieu of the planned traditional 
21 
capital investment.  The Companies may 
22 
optionally attempt to develop NPA projects for 
23 
those traditional capital investments which do 
24 
608


--- Page 86 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
69 
 
not meet all of the criteria, however, they 
1 
would not be obligated to do so. 
2 
Q. 
What screening criteria do the Companies 
3 
propose? 
4 
A. 
The Companies propose to separate projects into 
5 
two categories, large and small.  For small 
6 
projects, the Companies propose that they be 
7 
required to attempt to develop an NPA portfolio 
8 
for traditional projects with capital costs more 
9 
than $1 million and less than $3 million, which 
10 
would be designed to be completed within one 
11 
fiscal year.  For large projects, the Companies 
12 
propose to that they be required to pursue NPAs 
13 
for projects with capital costs greater than $3 
14 
million, which would be designed to be completed 
15 
over more than one fiscal year.  In the event 
16 
that these screening criteria need to be 
17 
adjusted, the Companies propose to make such 
18 
adjustments through a separate filing. 
19 
Q. 
Explain the Companies’ proposed eligibility 
20 
requirements for resources participating in NPA 
21 
projects. 
22 
A. 
The Companies propose that a resource must be a 
23 
commercially-proven technology that provides 
24 
609


--- Page 87 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
70 
 
load relief coincident with the system need, and 
1 
upon which the Companies can rely on to delay or 
2 
replace the need for the applicable traditional 
3 
capital project. 
4 
Q. 
Provide an overview of the Companies’ proposed 
5 
NPA Incentive Mechanism. 
6 
A. 
The Companies propose a shareholder incentive 
7 
equal to 30 percent of identified societal net 
8 
benefits, plus or minus 50 percent of portfolio 
9 
cost savings or overruns, respectively.  The 
10 
Companies propose to cap the incentive at no 
11 
greater than 50 percent of the societal net 
12 
benefits, and propose an incentive floor of $0.  
13 
The Companies propose slightly different 
14 
mechanisms for large and small NPA projects.  
15 
The Companies propose to use a streamlined 
16 
process for small projects based on the 
17 
calculation of the incentives on a per-Dth basis 
18 
to recognize the more rapid speed of development 
19 
for these smaller projects.  The Companies 
20 
propose a more rigorous process for large 
21 
projects, which would recognize the longer lead 
22 
time available for projects of this magnitude.  
23 
For both small and large projects, the Companies 
24 
610


--- Page 88 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
71 
 
propose adjustment mechanisms to modify the 
1 
available incentive if an updated needs 
2 
assessment determines that more or less load 
3 
relief is required to achieve deferral or 
4 
replacement of the traditional infrastructure, 
5 
or if the NPA project will not be able to defer 
6 
the infrastructure as intended. 
7

Provide an overview of the Companies’ proposals 
8 
regarding recovery of NPA project and incentive 
9

The Companies cost recovery proposals are 
11

16 
in the Delivery Rate Adjustment surcharge; and 
17 
(4) roll-in of unrecovered amortized NPA costs 
18 
into base rates during the Companies’ next rate 
19 
proceedings. 
20 
Q. 
What modifications do the Companies propose to 
21 
their Capital Investment Reconciliation 
22 
Mechanism related to NPA projects? 
23 
A. 
To the extent that an NPA project results in the 
24 
611


--- Page 89 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

72 
 
Companies displacing a capital project reflected 
1 
in the Average Gas Plant in Service Balances

(balance), the Companies propose to reduce the

associated with that capital project.  The

In the event that those carrying charges are

Q. 
How do the Companies propose to allocate NPA 
13

14

The Companies propose to allocate NPA costs to 
15 
service classifications based on the type of 
16 
traditional solution the NPA project would defer

or eliminate, roughly on the basis of a

20

22

23 
based on each SC’s contribution to Total Gas

612

Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
73 
 
Deliveries.

The Companies propose to amortize NPA project 
4

5

6 
surcharge as they are incurred.  For earned NPA-

7 
Q. 
What is the Panel’s general philosophy with 
8 
regard to NPAs? 
9 
A. 
We recommend that the Companies be positioned to 
10 
begin responding to system needs with non-
11 
traditional solutions as quickly as possible.  
12 
Therefore, we recommend that the Commission 
13 
approve the necessary NPA development processes 
14 
and cost recovery mechanisms as part of these 
15 
proceedings.  Given the rapid pace of policy 
16 
developments in the gas business, however, we do 
17 
not recommend establishing any specific 
18 
shareholder incentive mechanism at this time, 
19 
especially since the Companies have yet to 
20 
identify any potential projects for which they 
21 
may pursue NPAs.  NPA projects, especially a 
22 
utility’s first such project, may take a year or 
23 
more to develop - even after the utility has 
24 
615


--- Page 93 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
76 
 
decided to pursue a particular NPA.  Any NPA 
1 
shareholder incentive mechanism approved as part 
2 
of these cases runs the risk of potentially 
3 
being out of step with the then-current 
4 
Commission policy by the time the Companies have 
5 
developed an actual NPA project proposal. 
6 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any specific recommendations 
7 
regarding the Companies’ NPA-related proposals? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  We recommend that the Commission approve 
9 
the Companies’ NPA-related proposals with the 
10 
exception of the Companies’ proposed NPA 
11 
Incentive Mechanism.  Instead of approving the 
12 
Companies’ proposed NPA Incentive Mechanism, the 
13 
Commission should allow the Companies to file a 
14 
petition for an appropriate shareholder 
15 
incentive mechanism concomitantly with the 
16 
Implementation Plan for the Companies’ first NPA 
17 
project. 
18 
Q. 
Are there benefits to approving NPA development 
19 
criteria and cost recovery mechanisms in these 
20 
proceedings, but delaying approval of a 
21 
shareholder incentive mechanism? 
22 
A. 
Yes.  Approving the NPA development criteria and 
23 
cost recovery mechanisms in these proceedings 
24 
616


--- Page 94 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SEAMP 
 
 
77 
 
will provide the Companies with regulatory 
1 
certainty with regard to their ability to 
2 
recover NPA costs.  This will eliminate barriers 
3 
so that the Companies can begin seeking NPA 
4 
opportunities as soon as possible.  While 
5 
delaying approval of a specific shareholder 
6

it will ensure that the shareholder incentive 
9

11

12 
that time.

THE WITNESS:  Thank you, Judge.  You 
can see that we examined the number of basis points 
and required improvement rates for the energy 
efficiency E.A.M. metrics that are currently enforced 
at O&R, Central Hudson and Niagara Mohawk.  We also 
generally looked at things like the number of basis 
points available for each of those utilities that the 
commission has already approved. 
BY MR. RIGBERG:  (Cont'g.) 
Q.   Thank you.  Now, when you use 
that term improvement rates, what -- what does that 
mean? 
A.   Specifically, on Exhibit 
S.E.A.M.P.-3, we examined the difference between the 
maximum target for the energy efficiency programmatic 
E.A.M. at O&R, Central Hudson and Niagara Mohawk 
compared to the minimum target for that same metric 
at each of those utilities and determined a 
percentage of difference between the maximum target 
and the minimum target.  We then used that percentage 
difference that the commission has already approved 
for targets in those cases to inform what our maximum 
number of basis points might be under the energy 
efficiency share of the savings metric in this case.

Q.   And did you set the tar -- your 
tar -- the targets in this case higher based on the 
outcomes in the other cases, or – lower, or the same? 
A.   (Cully)  I would say that our 
considerations and target setting in this case are 
informed by the other cases.  We always strive to 
provide targets that are reasonable for each utility.  
That neither provide an incentive for doing nothing, 
nor are wholly unachievable. 
However, the answer is more 
complicated in this case.  Because in the case of 
KEDLI and KEDNY in the present proceeding, we are 
recommending a share-the-savings type metric for the 
energy-efficiency programs instead of the incremental 
dekatherm of energy efficiency achieved metric in the 
three prior cases. 
The difference is essentially that the 
other metrics have defined targets and basis points 
available with each of those targets.  Whereas, as we 
described in our pre-filed testimony, direct 
testimony rather, the share-the-savings metric is 
based on the difference in dollar per lifetime 
M.M.B.T.U. run rates of the energy efficiency 
program.

Q. 
Did the March 2018 ETIP Order include other 
1

2 
ETIP programs? 
3 
A. 
Yes.  In the March 2018 ETIP Order, the 
4 
Commission: (1) adjusted each utility’s targets 
5 
from net to gross savings and emphasized the 
6 
importance of realized savings; (2) changed the 
7 
units of gas savings targets and reported 
8 
savings from Dth to Million British Thermal 
9 
Units (MMBtu); (3) required recovery of costs 
10

11

12 
(4) directed any utility EAM based on energy 
13 
efficiency programmatic performance be 
14

15 
savings; and, (5) required each utility to 
16 
evolve its ETIP into a System Energy Efficiency 
17 
Plan (SEEP). 
18 
Q. 
Please explain the Commission’s directive to 
19 
shift to gross savings targets. 
20 
A. 
Previous Commission policy required that both 
21 
energy efficiency targets, and savings reported 
22 
against those targets, be expressed on a net 
23 
basis, accounting for the effects of free-
24 
659


--- Page 137 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SESP 
 
 
14 
 
ridership and spillover by applying a default 
1 
net-to-gross ratio of 0.90.  In recognition of 
2 
specific barriers that make it difficult to 
3 
meaningfully calculate accurate net-to-gross 
4 
ratios, the Commission determined that, going 
5 
forward, goals should be established, and 
6 
savings reported, on a gross basis.  The March 
7 
2018 ETIP Order defined gross savings as the 
8 
change in energy consumption and/or demand that 
9 
results directly from program-related actions 
10 
taken by participants in a program, regardless 
11 
of why they participated. 
12

Tracker surcharge mechanism into base delivery

rates during the first rate proceeding for that

savings.  The Commission’s requirement for

which are critical to expanded energy efficiency

and the attainment of the State’s greenhouse gas 
20 
emission reduction objectives.  The direct 
21 
testimony of the Staff EAM Panel includes

offset the ETIP energy efficiency budgets. 
3 
Q. 
Please explain what is meant by “unspent ETIP 
4 
funds.” 
5 
A. 
Unspent ETIP funds are those funds that the 
6 
Companies collected from customers to pay for 
7 
energy efficiency programs offered during the 
8 
previous ETIP period but that the Companies have 
9 
not spent as of the beginning of the Rate Year. 
10 
Q. 
How does interest accrue on NYSERDA CEF 
11 
collections? 
12 
A. 
The NYSERDA CEF collections are collected by the 
13 
Companies through the SBC surcharge.  NYSERDA 
14 
relies on a quarterly BAYG approach to obtain 
15 
the CEF funding from the utilities.  Under this 
16 
methodology, KEDLI and KEDNY collect their 
17 
authorized share of NYSERDA’s CEF budget on an 
18 
ongoing basis, but only transfer their 
19 
proportional share of NYSERDA’s actual 
20 
expenditures to NYSERDA at the end of each 
21 
quarter.  This approach results in the Companies 
22 
accruing a balance of CEF funds.  Further, in 
23 
accordance with the Commission’s Order 
24 
674


--- Page 152 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SESP 
 
 
29 
 
Authorizing the Clean Energy Fund Framework (CEF 
1 
Order), issued on January 21, 2016 in Case 14-M-
2 
0094, the Companies are required to accrue 
3 
carrying charges on this balance using the Other 
4 
Customer Provided Capital Rate. 
5 
Q. 
What does the Panel recommend regarding the use 
6 
of unspent EEPS and ETIP funds and EEPS and BAYG 
7 
interest to offset the energy efficiency 
8

9 
A. 
We recommend that the Companies use the combined 
10 
total of the EEPS and ETIP unspent funds along 
11 
with the EEPS and NYSERDA BAYG interest to 
12 
offset the energy efficiency budgets over a

14 
summary of the unspent EEPS and ETIP funds and 
15 
EEPS and BAYG accrued interest and our 
16 
recommended use of those funds. 
17

KEDLI HEAT Program 
16 
Q. 
Please provide the background of the KEDLI HEAT 
17 
Program. 
18 
A. 
The Commission’s January 21, 2016 Order in the 
19 
Clean Energy Fund proceeding, Case 14-M-0094, 
20 
required that NYSERDA operate its programs in a 
21 
fuel-neutral manner and, to facilitate this 
22 
fuel-neutral approach, established a policy that 
23 
eliminates collections from non-electric 
24 
676


--- Page 154 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SESP 
 
 
31 
 
customers.  As KEDLI customers receive electric 
1 
service from the Long Island Power Authority, 
2 
which does not contribute to NYSERDA programs, 
3 
KEDLI customers are not eligible for NYSERDA 
4 
programs, including NYSERDA’s EmPower Program 
5 
for low-income customers.  In order to avoid a 
6 
gap in energy efficiency services to low-income 
7 
customers in KEDLI’s territory, the Commission 
8 
required NYSERDA and KEDLI to pursue 
9 
alternatives for 2017 and thereafter.  KEDLI 
10 
developed the HEAT program as its EmPower 
11 
replacement program and has been implementing it 
12 
since 2017. 
13 
Q. 
Please describe KEDLI’s HEAT program. 
14 
A. 
The HEAT program is an energy efficiency program 
15 
designed to serve low-income customers.  The 
16 
program consists of four tiers: Tier 1 includes 
17 
customer outreach, education, and engagement to 
18 
educate customers on the benefits of energy 
19 
efficiency and encourage participation in Tier 2 
20 
measure offerings; Tier 2 consists of a home 
21 
energy audit and direct installation of certain 
22 
energy efficiency measures; Tier 3 entails the 
23 
installation of additional energy efficiency 
24 
677


--- Page 155 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SESP 
 
 
32 
 
measures identified in the Tier 2 home energy 
1 
audit; and Tier 4 includes the installation of 
2 
custom energy efficiency measures that may 
3 
require extensive work on the home, including 
4 
the preparation of space needed to accommodate 
5 
energy efficiency equipment. 
6 
Q. 
Does KEDLI propose any changes to the HEAT 
7 
program? 
8 
A. 
Yes.  On page 29 of the direct testimony of the 
9 
Future of Heat Panel, KEDLI proposes to increase 
10 
the annual budget from $1.9 million to $2.5 
11 
million.  KEDLI also proposes to expand the 
12 
program to include eligible moderate income 
13 
customers whose residences do not have central 
14 
air conditioning. 
15 
Q. 
Does the Panel support KEDLI’s proposed 
16 
expansions of the HEAT Program to moderate-
17 
income customers? 
18 
A. 
Yes, we support increasing the budget to $2.5 
19 
million and expanding the program to eligible 
20 
moderate income customers. 
21 
Q. 
Is the HEAT program a part of KEDLI’s energy 
22 
efficiency portfolio? 
23 
A. 
No.  Currently, KEDLI implements this program as 
24 
678


--- Page 156 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SESP 
 
 
33 
 
a standalone program that operates outside of 
1 
the energy efficiency portfolio. 
2

3

4 
A. 
Yes.  We recommend that KEDLI incorporate the 
5

DEMAND RESPONSE 
19 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ Demand Response 
20 
(DR) programs. 
21 
A. 
On page 42 of the direct testimony of the Future 
22 
of Heat Panel, the Companies state that they 
23 
have two DR programs.  The first is the 
24 
686


--- Page 164 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SESP 
41
Interruptible and Temperature Control (IT/TC) 
1 
Service that offers a reduced delivery rate for 
2 
customers that interrupt their natural gas 
3 
service when demand peaks.  The IT/TC service 
4 
has two pricing tiers that customers are placed 
5 
into based on the customer’s fuel switching 
6 
capabilities.  The Companies do not propose 
7 
changes to IT/TC service in this proceeding. 
8 
Q.
What is the second DR program? 
9 
A.
The second DR program is currently a 
10 
Demonstration Project providing market-based 
11 
payment to commercial firm customers who commit 
12 
to minimum gas usage reductions during peak 
13 
events, based on their performance during 
14 
specified DR events.  This project was approved 
15 
as part of the 2016 Rate Order, issued on 
16 
December 16, 2016, in which the Commission 
17 
adopted a Joint Proposal and established rate 
18 
plans for the Companies in Cases 16-G-0058 and 
19 
16-G-0059.  Our testimony in these proceedings
20 
will focus on the Companies’ proposals related 
21 
to this program, and we will refer to this 
22 
program as the Performance-based Gas DR Program. 
23 
Q.
How do customers currently participate in the 
24 
687


--- Page 165 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SESP 
 
 
42 
 
demonstration-scale program? 
1 
A. 
Currently, customers enroll load relief 
2 
associated with direct utility control of 
3 
specific equipment within customers’ premises, 
4 
with an associated bid price.  The Companies use 
5 
a competitive selection process based on the 
6 
load relief and price proposed in the bids 
7 
received from customers.  The Companies develop 
8 
a load relief portfolio seeking to maximize load 
9 
relief and minimize costs.  Thereafter, during a 
10 
load relief event, KEDNY and KEDLI directly shut 
11 
off enrolled customer equipment for the duration 
12 
of the Event.  A more complete description of 
13 
the current Gas Demand Response Demonstration 
14 
Project is available as part of the Companies’ 
15 
implementation plan filings made on July 27, 
16 
2017 in Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059. 
17 
Q. 
Do KEDNY and KEDLI propose changes to the 
18 
Performance-based Gas DR Program? 
19 
A. 
Yes.  On pages 44 and 45 of the direct testimony 
20 
of the Future of Heat Panel, the Companies 
21 
propose scaling the project up from 
22 
demonstration size and including the costs of 
23 
the Performance-based Gas DR Program in base 
24 
688


--- Page 166 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SESP 
 
 
43 
 
rates.  Additionally, the Companies propose 
1 
making three operational changes which will 
2 
impact participant responsiveness to peak 
3 
events.

Q. 
What is the first operational change that the 
5 
Companies propose? 
6 
A. 
The Companies propose certain new requirements 
7 
for customer sizes and proposed load relief 
8 
amounts.  Specifically, the Companies to require 
9 
that firm customers meet a minimum annual demand 
10

12

13 
pledged by participants must be at least 10

percent of either the customer’s gas usage

during a peak period or the sum of such 
16

17 
Specifically, KEDNY proposes that firm customers 
18 
must consume at least 6,000 Dth per year, and 
19 
must pledge a minimum load relief amount of 2 
20 
Dth/hour during events.  Similarly, KEDLI 
21 
proposes that firm customers must consume at 
22 
least 4,000 Dth per year, and must pledge a 
23 
minimum load relief amount of 2 Dth/hour during

689

Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SESP 
 
 
44 
 
events. 
1 
Q. 
What is the second operational change that the 
2 
Companies propose? 
3 
A. 
The Companies propose to require that, in order 
4 
to receive an incentive payment, participants 
5 
must reduce their gas demand for the entire 
6

7

What is the third operational change that the 
9

10

The Companies propose to impose a penalty when 
11

12

13

14

15 
events. 
16 
Q.

17 
A.

18 
Companies’ Performance-based Gas DR Program with 
19 
other Electric DR programs focused on reducing 
20 
Commercial and Industrial customers’ peak 
21 
demand, and would closely align with a similar 
22 
performance-based Gas Demand Response pilot 
23 
currently being operated by Con Edison.  
24 
690


--- Page 168 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SESP 
 
 
45 
 
Aligning KEDNY and KEDLI’s program rules with 
1 
other programs targeting the same customer 
2 
segment should help the Companies attract 
3 
customers that may already be familiar with the 
4 
Electric demand response programs, and allow 
5 
participants with greater flexibility regarding 
6 
the actions they take to reduce gas demand when 
7 
called upon since customers will be free to 
8 
choose how to provide load relief instead of 
9

10 
utility control.  Overall, we generally agree 
11 
that these changes will likely result in a more 
12

Q.

14 
significant degree of identical requirements for 
15

16 
Q.

17

18

22

23 
operational across the major electric investor-
24 
691


--- Page 169 ---

SESP 
 
 
46 
 
owned utilities since the summer of 2015, and 
1 
since 2009 at Con Edison, gas DR programs

KEDNY will be only the second such performance-
5 
based gas demand response program in New York 
6 
State.  Therefore, although the Commission has

8

For KEDLI, the CapEx costs are $107,000 during

692

SESP

during the Rate Year, and Data Years 1 and 2.

A.

8

9

16 
Q. 
Why does the Panel disagree with the Companies’ 
17 
proposal to include non-labor O&M costs of the 
18 
Program-based Gas Demand Response program in 
19 
base rates? 
20 
A. 
Non-labor O&M costs related to most demand 
21 
response programs are related to incentive costs 
22 
paid to participants.  As these incentive 
23 
payment costs can vary significantly since they 
24 
694


--- Page 172 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SESP 
 
 
49 
 
are based on the number of participants enrolled 
1 
in the program, the amount of load relief 
2 
enrolled in the program, the number of events 
3 
called in a given year, and, of course, 
4 
participant performance during those events.  
5 
These factors can lead to significant swings in 
6 
incentive payment costs from year to year, and 
7 
can be difficult to forecast.  Therefore, we 
8 
recommend that, instead of recovering these 
9 
costs in base rates, the Companies recover the 
10 
non-labor O&M costs as-incurred through the 
11 
Delivery Service Adjustment surcharge. 
12 
Q. 
Do other utilities recover the costs of their 
13 
demand response programs in a similar way? 
14 
A. 
Yes.  The other investor-owned utilities recover 
15 
the costs of their respective demand response 
16 
programs through a non-bypassable delivery 
17 
surcharge mechanism.  Applying this same cost 
18 
recovery mechanism to KEDNY and KEDLI would 
19 
bring their programs in line with other electric 
20 
and gas demand response programs statewide. 
21 
 
22

Clean Conversion Program 
1 
Q. 
What is the Clean Conversion Program? 
2 
A.

3 
Neighborhood Expansion Program with the Clean 
4 
Conversion Program.  Under the Clean Conversion 
5

8

environmentally friendly non-gas option, KEDLI

will offer an opportunity to switch from oil to 
11 
natural gas.  Under the Clean Conversion 
12 
Program, KEDLI will extend gas service to 
13 
customers under the same density and minimum 
14 
customer connections requirements established in 
15 
the Order Directing Investments and Allowing, In

with Incremental Capital Expenditures and

methodology, and that such methodology should

the New York State Department of Environmental 
23 
Conservation. 
24

--- Page 190 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SESP REBUTTAL 
 
 
6 
 
Q. 
Are there important facets of the CLCPA which 
1 
witness Ucar did not highlight in his testimony? 
2 
A. 
Yes, there are three main areas that witness 
3 
Ucar did not highlight which deserve further 
4 
discussion.  First, the CLCPA itself defines a 
5 
process for developing recommendations for how 
6 
to meet New York State’s climate obligations.  
7 
Second, while the CLCPA did require that the 
8 
Commission consider whether its decisions would 
9 
be consistent with meeting the GHG emissions 
10 
reductions requirements, it did not specify how 
11 
the Commission must make such discretionary 
12 
determinations.  Third, the CLCPA requires that 
13 
the New York State Department of Environmental 
14 
Conservation (NYSDEC), not individual utilities, 
15 
develop a statewide emissions inventory. 
16 
Q. 
What process does the CLCPA define for 
17 
developing recommended actions to meet New York 
18 
State’s climate requirements? 
19 
A. 
The CLCPA implements a new section of New York 
20 
State Environmental Conservation Law, §75, which 
21 
establishes the New York State Climate Action 
22 
Council (Climate Action Council), a body of 22 
23 
members ranging from the commissioners of 
24 
713


--- Page 191 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SESP REBUTTAL 
 
 
7 
 
various State agencies, the presidents of 
1 
various State authorities, the chairperson of 
2 
the Commission, as well as a number of other 
3 
appointees designated by the Governor and 
4 
members of the State Senate and Assembly.  The 
5 
CLCPA requires the Climate Action Council to 
6 
convene advisory panels and working groups to 
7 
help it deliberate, and, within two years of the 
8 
effective date of the Act, prepare and approve a 
9 
scoping plan outlining recommendations for 
10 
attaining emissions reductions of 40 percent of 
11 
1990 levels by the year 2030 and 85 percent 
12 
reductions of such levels by 2040, achieving 
13 
emissions reductions beyond the 85 percent level 
14 
required by 2040, as well as achieving net-zero 
15 
emissions from all sectors of the New York State 
16 
economy.  The CLCPA requires that the Climate 
17 
Action Council’s recommendations be incorporated 
18 
into the next State Energy Plan following the 
19 
development of such recommendations. 
20 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any specific recommendations 
21 
related to witness Ucar’s proposals?

A. 
Yes.  We have two recommendations.

Q. 
Please describe your first recommendation. 
24 
714


--- Page 192 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SESP REBUTTAL 
 
 
8 
 
A.

1

2 
direct testimony, which are consistent with 
3 
Commission policy, while the Climate Action 
4 
Council goes about its work as required by the

6

7

8 
with both Commission policy and the requirements

of the CLCPA, but should not pre-judge the 
10 
recommendations of the Climate Action Council

by the CLCPA.

15 
for comparing carbon emissions reductions to a 
16

18

Alternatively, the Commission could require the

24 
715


--- Page 193 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SESP REBUTTAL

ensure that the statewide GHG emissions report

and models developed by that agency are

5 
to establish a consistent methodology for use 
6 
across utility service territories.  The

8 
determine whether its actions are inconsistent 
9 
with the carbon emissions reduction goals set 
10 
forth in the CLCPA.  Thus, such decision is

beyond the scope of these rate proceedings,

which affect only two utilities and address

and water heating requirements.  On pages 13 and

District Heating Study 
22 
Q. 
Provide a summary of the City Policy Panel’s 
23

24 
719


--- Page 197 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SESP REBUTTAL 
 
 
13 
 
Heating Study. 
1 
A.

2 
the City Policy Panel proposes that the 
3

4 
to examine, pilot, and report on the potential 
5 
for utility-owned district geothermal systems to 
6

7

8 
study would require that the Companies hire a 
9 
consultant to examine the feasibility of 
10 
deploying district energy systems, identifying 
11 
and targeting areas of high LPP concentrations.  
12 
Phase two of the study would consist of 
13 
selecting ten to twenty high-potential or unique 
14 
use-case sites for further detailed assessment 
15 
and design, ultimately ranking such sites based 
16 
on potential benefits, costs, and feasibility.  
17 
Phase three would consist of a pilot program to 
18 
build and test district energy systems at 
19 
several of the sites identified and examined in 
20 
phases one and two.  Phase four of the study 
21 
would assess the scalability of the pilot 
22 
locations, examine the costs and benefits of 
23 
expanding the pilot, and would identify required 
24 
720


--- Page 198 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SESP REBUTTAL 
 
 
14 
 
changes to the pilot structure and regulatory 
1 
construct needed to increase adoption of 
2 
district energy systems. 
3 
Q. 
Did the City Policy Panel provide a forecast of 
4 
District Heating Study costs, or propose a way 
5 
to pay for the study? 
6 
A. 
While the City Policy Panel did not provide a 
7 
forecast of the costs related to the study, it 
8 
did propose that the Commission consider changes 
9 
to the regulatory model to allow the avoided 
10 
costs of LPP replacements to be used to offset 
11 
the capital costs of district energy systems. 
12 
Q. 
Does the Panel agree with the City Policy 
13 
Panel’s proposed District Heat Study? 
14 
A. 
Partially.  While we agree that displacing LPP 
15 
replacement capital projects is attractive as a 
16 
potential area where gas capital expenditures 
17 
could be avoided using geothermal heat pumps or 
18 
other technologies, we disagree with the City’s 
19 
implication that utility ownership of shared 
20 
loop fields should be the default solution. 
21 
Q. 
Why should utility ownership of shared loop 
22 
fields not be the default option? 
23 
A. 
First, as noted earlier, Staff Counsel has 
24 
721


--- Page 199 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SESP REBUTTAL 
 
 
15 
 
advised us that there may be legal issues with 
1 
utility ownership of non-gas assets.  Second, 
2 
setting aside those legal issues for the sake of 
3 
argument, we see utility ownership of geothermal 
4 
loop fields and/or heat pumps as a last resort 
5 
in the event that the private market is 
6 
unwilling or unable to provide a cost-effective 
7 
solution.  Whether market conditions warrant 
8 
utility ownership of geothermal systems should 
9 
be tested under the same criteria as utility 
10 
ownership of any other type of distributed 
11 
energy resource (DER). 
12 
Q. 
Has the Commission adopted a set of criteria for 
13 
utility ownership of DER? 
14 
A. 
Yes, the Commission’s February 26, 2015 Order 
15 
Adopting Regulatory Policy Framework and 
16 
Implementation Plan in Case 14-M-0101 (Track One 
17 
Order) provides such criteria.  On page 68 of 
18 
the Track One Order, the Commission establishes 
19 
that utilities will not be allowed to own DER 
20 
assets as a general rule, unless markets have 
21 
had an opportunity to provide service and have 
22 
failed to do so in a cost-effective manner.  On 
23 
page 70 of the Track One Order, the Commission 
24 
722


--- Page 200 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SESP REBUTTAL 
 
 
16 
 
lays out four exceptions to the general rule 
1 
under which utility ownership would be allowed 
2 
where: (1) procurement of DER has been solicited 
3 
to meet a system need, and the utility has 
4 
demonstrated that competitive alternatives 
5 
proposed by non-utility parties are clearly 
6 
inadequate or more costly than a traditional 
7 
utility infrastructure alternative; (2) a 
8 
project consists of electric energy storage 
9 
integrated into electric distribution grid 
10 
infrastructure; (3) a project will enable low 
11 
and moderate income (LMI) customers to benefit 
12 
from DER where markets are not likely to satisfy 
13 
the need; and (4) a project is part of a 
14 
Demonstration Project. 
15 
Q. 
Has the City Policy Panel demonstrated a market 
16 
failure, or that its proposed District Energy 
17 
Study and pilot are exempt from the general rule 
18 
prohibiting utility ownership of DER? 
19 
A. 
No, it has not. 
20 
Q. 
Should the District Energy Study be considered a 
21 
Demonstration Project? 
22 
A. 
No.  The Track One Order provided Demonstration 
23 
Project authorization and budgets for Electric 
24 
723


--- Page 201 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SESP REBUTTAL 
 
 
17 
 
utilities only.  As a procedural matter, utility 
1

2

3 
clearly, by our testimony on this matter, 
4 
approval by Staff of a utility-owned district 
5

6

8

10

12 
not supporting the Companies’ proposals to 
13

17 
A.

1

8 
III.  
Structure of and Services Provided by National Grid IT  
9 
Q. 
Please describe the role of the IT function at National Grid. 
10 
A. 
IT services range from critical gas transmission/distribution support 
11 
systems to standard office desktop applications.  These services underpin 
12 
the safe, reliable, and secure physical and commercial operation of 
13 
KEDNY and KEDLI’s gas distribution businesses.  IT also provides a 
14 
suite of software applications that serve the needs of customers and allow 
15 
for the effective management and operation of KEDNY and KEDLI.  
16 
National Grid operates these applications on hardware systems that are 
17 
centrally located as well as distributed to employees in the form of 
18 
personal computers and other devices.  IT also provides KEDNY and 
19 
KEDLI with asset and work management systems that provide the 
20 
administrative support required to manage gas service. 
21 
819


--- Page 297 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
10 of 55 
 
The utilization of information technology is an important tool for utilities 
1 
to improve the efficiency of energy delivery networks, and will be a 
2 
central enabler for all parts of network operation.  Reducing energy usage, 
3 
balancing energy demands, and providing more reliable energy delivery 
4 
can only be achieved by advancements in technology.  All this change 
5 
comes with a new regulatory landscape, and changes in National Grid’s 
6 
risk portfolio.  
7 
 
8 
To prepare for current and future needs, the IT organization is 
9 
transitioning from being primarily an order taker and service delivery 
10 
organization to a key partner with the business in developing strategies to 
11 
address the most pressing business needs.  This has resulted in National 
12 
Grid reskilling its IT workforce to become more customer focused and 
13 
changing our engagement model with suppliers. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Please summarize the structure of National Grid’s IT organization. 
16 
A. 
The IT organization is structured to ensure accountability for achieving its 
17 
goals.  The organization includes four CIO positions (US CIO, UK CIO, 
18 
Group Functions CIO, and Ventures CIO) who report to the Group Chief 
19 
Information and Digital Officer.  The US CIO is fully responsible for US 
20 
IT operations including developing the current and future capabilities and 
21 
820


--- Page 298 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
11 of 55 
 
skills of the teams that serve National Grid’s US businesses.  The US CIO 
1 
is a member of the executive management team overseeing US business 
2 
operations and is the face of IT for National Grid’s US business, as well as 
3 
the conduit for presenting business requirements to the overall IT 
4 
organization.   
5 
 
6 
 
Reporting to the US CIO are five US IT Business Partners (i.e., Electric, 
7 
Gas, Transmission and Capital Delivery, Customer, and Finance and 
8 
Business Services).  The Business Partners sit on the management teams 
9 
of their respective business segments and are responsible for the 
10 
relationship with the business stakeholders.  They also are responsible for 
11 
long-term planning, development of annual budgets, and ensuring IT 
12 
programs and projects deliver the promised business benefits and enable 
13 
the businesses to deliver their commitments to customers.  
14 
 
 
15 
The Business Partners work with National Grid’s IT Delivery Center 
16 
leaders to ensure that resources with appropriate skill sets are available to 
17 
meet the forecast demands of National Grid’s businesses.  Delivery Center 
18 
leaders are accountable for providing services at agreed levels of cost, 
19 
time, and quality by developing and implementing common ways of 
20 
821


--- Page 299 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
12 of 55 
 
working across the US.  The IT organization contains the following 
1 
Delivery Centers: Strategy and Innovation; Architecture; Solution 
2 
Delivery; Infrastructure and Operations; Digital Risk and Security; 
3 
Commercial and Vendor Management; and Data Management.  A 
4 
simplified graphic depiction of National Grid’s IT organization is 
5 
provided in Exhibit __ (ITP-1).  
6 
  
7 
Q. 
What services does the IT function provide to the Companies? 
8 
A. 
National Grid’s IT function provides, maintains, and manages the 
9 
computer 
hardware, 
software, 
security 
(cyber 
and 
physical) 
10 
telecommunications, and other related infrastructure, systems, and services 
11 
for National Grid’s business operations across all National Grid service 
12 
territories.   
13 
  
14 
Q. 
Please explain National Grid’s approach to IT service delivery. 
15 
A. 
As described earlier, National Grid has a robust IT organizational structure 
16 
designed to effectively manage the IT needs of the entire business.  To 
17 
deliver on the service needs of the business, National Grid’s IT 
18 
organization utilizes several external partners, each of which performs a 
19 
specific IT function.  These functions include: 
20 
822


--- Page 300 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
13 of 55 
 
• Application development and maintenance: This includes a full 
1 
range of application services, such as developing new applications 
2 
and day-to-day support of existing applications. 
3 
• Internet, collaboration, and e-mail: This function manages email, 
4 
web conferencing, instant messaging and collaboration tools, such 
5 
as 
SharePoint, 
operated 
on 
vendor-owned 
and 
hosted 
6 
infrastructure. 
7 
• Networks and communications: This function manages a single 
8 
network service that consolidates National Grid’s Local Area 
9 
Network (“LAN”), Wide Area Network (“WAN”), telephony, and 
10 
video and audio conferencing. 
11 
• Data center and client services/enterprise services: This function 
12 
provides data center services (e.g., servers, data storage); manages 
13 
hardware, software, and storage located in data centers; back-up 
14 
capability and disaster recovery services; and client services, such

as the provision and support of end user devices (e.g., laptops), and 
16 
deployment of the operating systems and applications that run on 
17 
those devices. 
18 
• Managed print: This function manages support services for a 
19 
refreshed and standardized fleet of print devices, enabling 
20 
increased security for printing, copying, faxing, and scanning.

823

Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
14 of 55 
 
These core IT services enable the effective operation of the entire 
1 
organization.   
2 
 
3 
Q.  
Please describe how the Company develops the IT investment plan. 
4 
A.  
To determine which projects to include in the IT investment plan, IT 
5 
engages in an iterative process that involves collaboration with 
6 
representatives 
of 
the 
various 
jurisdictions 
(e.g., 
New 
York, 
7 
Massachusetts, Rhode Island, and FERC) and business units (e.g., Gas, 
8 
Electric, Transmission and Capital Delivery) to identify the highest 
9 
priority projects.  IT collaborates with the jurisdictions and business units 
10 
to assess infrastructure requirements, sequencing, resource availability and 
11 
capability, size and impact of investment requests, and the overall benefits 
12 
of the investments.  First, IT considers in-flight multi-year investments 
13 
and identifies the amount of work remaining and the delivery timeline for 
14 
those projects.  Second, IT adds new investments needed to comply with 
15 
legal or regulatory requirements/mandates or other critical corporate 
16 
objectives.  Third, IT adds projects to address IT infrastructure - primarily 
17 
investments to upgrade/replace/add functionality to maintain reliability in 
18 
systems and establish foundational capabilities required for future 
19 
operational needs of the businesses.  Finally, IT works through the 
20 
Business Partners to prioritize project requests of the business.  After this 
21 
824


--- Page 302 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
15 of 55 
 
process, IT distributes a draft investment plan to the business units, 
1 
jurisdictional representatives, and other key stakeholders for feedback on 
2 
the plan.  A final investment plan review occurs each fiscal year (“FY”) 
3 
with the Jurisdictional Presidents and business unit leadership.  Once the 
4 
Jurisdictional Presidents and business unit executives review and approve 
5 
the investment plan, the consolidated financial information from the plan, 
6 
which is organized by jurisdiction and operating company, is incorporated 
7 
with the IT operation and maintenance (“O&M”) requirements into the IT 
8 
budget.  The IT budget is then reviewed and approved by the US CIO, 
9 
Global CIO, and Corporate Finance, and incorporated into a consolidated 
10 
National Grid budget.

IV. 
IT Cost Allocation and Historic Test Year Costs 
13 
Q.  
How are the costs of IT projects and investments charged to KEDNY 
14 
and KEDLI? 
15 
A.  
IT capital projects and investments that are shared investments across 
16 
operating companies are implemented and owned by National Grid 
17 
Service Company.  The costs of these investments are allocated to 
18 
KEDNY and KEDLI (and the other operating companies that utilize 
19 
and/or benefit from the investments) in the form of rent expense.  Exhibit 
20 
__ (ITP-2) provides the allocation factors utilized to determine the forecast 
21 
825


--- Page 303 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
16 of 55 
 
annual rent expense for the IT projects planned for the Rate Year and Data 
1 
Years.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
How are the costs of IT capital projects recovered? 
4 
A. 
The costs of IT capital projects are amortized over a period of years.  The 
5 
amortization period is determined by the average service lives of the 
6 
assets.  In general, many of the IT-related assets on the books of the 
7 
National Grid Service Company are either software intangibles or portable 
8 
general plant equipment that is being depreciated/amortized and recovered 
9 
over a period equal to their planned usefulness or expected lives.  The 
10 
actual physical replacement of assets is determined by business decisions 
11 
to maintain, refresh, replace, and/or decommission assets, and that 
12 
evaluation is conducted based on variables such as operational risk, cost to 
13 
be incurred, and the availability of replacement options.  Recovery of 
14 
hardware and equipment and amortization of software intangible assets 
15 
will approximate the useful life of the new system, taking into 
16 
consideration technological and functional obsolescence.   
17

A. 
Technology Modernization 
8 
Q. 
Please describe the Technology Modernization Program. 
9 
A. 
The Technology Modernization Program is an investment program aimed 
10 
at updating National Grid’s IT infrastructure and applications to enable 
11 
continued reliable and efficient operation.  The key investment areas 
12 
include: the infrastructure and network that are used to compute, store, and 
13 
transfer data and the business applications that support National Grid’s 
14 
Gas and Electric Operations. National Grid’s long-term legacy systems 
15 
have served it well in the past.  However, the IT infrastructure and 
16 
applications estate has become aged and increasingly prone to failure, 
17 
resulting in costly disruptions to business operations.  Further, because of 
18 
the fast pace of technology change, many of the applications and 
19 
infrastructure components within the IT estate are no longer supported by 
20 
the vendors, such that some software applications are currently supported 
21 
830


--- Page 308 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
21 of 55 
 
by application maintenance vendors on a “best endeavors” basis only, and 
1 
some replacement hardware is no longer available from the manufacturer 
2 
and must be sourced from secondary markets.  There are risks with 
3 
operating critical applications and infrastructure that are aged and no 
4 
longer 
supported 
including 
reduced 
reliability, 
resiliency, 
and 
5 
maintainability; operational inefficiencies due to manual workarounds; 
6 
data security issues; decreased service levels and vendor support; and 
7 
potential system failure.  Additionally, National Grid’s current IT systems 
8 
and platforms are heavily customized, lack the flexibility to accommodate 
9 
new business needs, and will not support near term change and regulatory 
10 
initiatives.    
11 
 
12 
Investment to replace and upgrade applications and infrastructure 
13 
components will improve the ability to respond to evolving business and 
14 
market demands and improve operation of the energy system in the future 
15 
so that it runs more efficiently, reliably, and safely.  Specifically, the IT 
16 
Technology Modernization Program will deliver the following benefits: 
17 
• With respect to applications, the program will:  
18 
o upgrade applications at end of life to current and supported 
19 
versions 
20 
o retire at-risk applications 
21 
o consolidate core systems to simplify the application landscape 
22 
831


--- Page 309 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
22 of 55 
 
o develop architecture that allows reuse and easy application 
1 
“plug-in”  
2 
o reduce cyber risk through upgrades to operating systems and/or 
3 
applications 
4 
o implement new tools to manage applications and automate 
5 
business processing 
6 
o ensure applications are upgraded in accordance with 
7 
application roadmaps   
8 
• With respect to infrastructure, the program will: 
9 
o remediate high risk, failure-prone assets to ensure business 
10 
continuity 
11 
o improve network capabilities to enable cloud and mobile 
12 
adoption, improve cyber-security, and ease maintainability 
13 
o modernize the end user environment to provide new and 
14 
flexible collaboration capabilities (internally and externally) 
15 
o adopt strategic data centers to mitigate risk and enable robust, 
16 
scalable infrastructure solutions

Q. 
Please describe the Application Rationalization initiative that is part 
1 
of the Technology Modernization program. 
2 
A. 
National Grid has a complex application landscape, with over 500 
3

4 
consolidation and decommissioning.  Many of these applications have 
5 
reached, or are reaching, end-of-life and are in dire need of upgrade or 
6 
replacement.  This is impacting the organization by creating needs for 
7 
intense manual processing (e.g., manual bill creation, US field force time 
8 
entry).  To maintain existing efficiencies, it will be important to 
9 
consolidate National Grid’s application portfolio.

12

14 
refresh and updates to the Data Center infrastructure to ensure that 
15 
services continue to be appropriately secure and maintain the reliability 
16 
required by the business.   It is estimated that approximately 70 percent of 
17 
the infrastructure is no longer supported and storage capability is nearing 
18 
capacity.  This investment is needed to maintain the operating 
19 
environment at a supportable level, and represents a positive step change 
20 
in reducing infrastructure nearing the end of its useful life.
