B. 
Cyber Security and Physical Security  
11 
Q. 
How are KEDNY and KEDLI addressing cyber security threats? 
12 
A. 
In 2010, National Grid established the Digital Risk and Security 
13 
organization within the IT organization to protect National Grid’s energy 
14 
networks, IT systems, and confidential company and customer information 
15 
from cyber security threats.  Digital Risk and Security is responsible for 
16 
identifying and managing cyber risk, development and delivery of secure 
17 
solutions, identifying, evaluating, and addressing known and emerging 
18 
threats against National Grid’s environments, and implementing a 
19 
program of cyber security initiatives to proactively identify and protect 
20 
National Grid from emerging threats.   
21 
835


--- Page 313 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
26 of 55 
 
 
1 
The Digital Risk and Security organization established a Cyber Security 
2 
Program outlining a multi-year investment strategy, continuously 
3 
evaluated based on emerging threats, to enhance National Grid’s cyber 
4 
capabilities.  The aim of the Cyber Security Program is to address cyber 
5 
security threats to the Enterprise, Critical Network Infrastructure (“CNI”), 
6 
and Operational Technology network systems and environments. The 
7 
Cyber Security Program focuses on cyber capabilities and the 
8 
development of safeguards to address key threat areas, including:  
9 
• Unauthorized Access / Insider Attack 
10 
• System Availability / Malfunction 
11 
• Malware / Virus Attack 
12 
• Advanced Persistent Threat / External Attack 
13 
• Data Leakage / Loss / Privacy 
14 
• Regulatory Non-Compliance 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Does National Grid work with others on cyber security issues? 
17 
A. 
Yes.  National Grid leverages industry and governmental agencies to stay 
18 
informed of emerging threats.  National Grid participates in government 
19 
information sharing programs that support threat identification and risk 
20 
reduction.  National Grid collaborates with government agencies such as 
21 
836


--- Page 314 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
27 of 55 
 
the Federal Bureau of Investigation, Electricity Information Sharing and 
1 
Analysis Center, and the US Department of Homeland Security, through 
2 
the National Cybersecurity and Communications Integration Center.  
3 
These agencies provide situational awareness on security threats, 
4 
including alerts, near-real time cyber threat indicator sharing, remediation, 
5 
incident response, and other security specific resources.  
6 
 
7 
Q. 
What is National Grid’s plan to address cyber security in the Rate 
8 
Year and Data Years? 
9 
A. 
National Grid’s approach to cyber security continues to evolve to keep 
10 
pace with changing threats across the entire enterprise.  Digital Risk and 
11 
Security’s strategy aims to enable the strategic goals and objectives of the 
12 
business, deliver the appropriate safeguards to protect the organization’s 
13 
most critical assets, ensure a robust cyber security workforce and first line 
14 
of defense, and establish risk management and governance frameworks to 
15 
appropriately identify, manage, mitigate, and communicate cyber risk and 
16 
ensure proportionate measures are delivered to address the most pertinent 
17 
areas of risk. 
18 
  
19 
Q. 
What steps does National Grid take to review its cyber security 
20 
program to ensure it is meeting its objective? 
21 
837


--- Page 315 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
28 of 55 
 
A. 
National Grid continuously reviews and updates the program to enhance 
1 
the ability to protect enterprise and CNI systems and operations as 
2 
technology changes and new threats emerge.  National Grid will continue 
3 
to advance its cyber security capabilities to address threats while also 
4 
making investments focused on improving the cyber security posture of 
5 
operational technology systems.  
6 
 
7 
National Grid has adopted the National Institute of Standards and 
8 
Technology (“NIST”) Cyber Security Framework (“CSF”) as the 
9 
methodology to manage cyber risk and associated mitigation plans and 
10 
improve its cyber posture.  The NIST CSF provides a structured approach 
11 
to identifying and managing risk, providing a comprehensive range of 
12 
security controls and measures that when implemented will decrease the 
13 
likelihood of threats of unauthorized access, compromise, disruption, and 
14 
destruction to core systems and infrastructure. The Cyber Security 
15 
program will ensure continued deployment of capabilities to enterprise, 
16 
CNI, and operational technology systems, in alignment with the NIST 
17 
CSF, to predict and detect attacks, respond effectively to incidents through 
18 
continued investment in defense-in-depth architectures, and controls to 
19 
prevent unauthorized attacks and compromise of critical environments. 
20 
 
21

Q. 
What is the purpose of National Grid’s physical security investments? 
12 
A. 
National Grid operates electric and gas energy infrastructure that could be 
13 
subject to physical attack.  Physical security threats include the risk that 
14 
unauthorized individuals will attempt to enter the Companies’ facilities 
15 
with the intent of vandalizing property, equipment or facilities, theft of 
16 
tangible or intellectual property, or damage of critical equipment.  
17 
National Grid’s physical security measures are intended to mitigate the 
18 
risk of such threats by deploying enhanced security systems across the 
19 
Companies’ networks and facilities to detect and deter physical attacks.   
20 
 
21 
841


--- Page 319 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
32 of 55 
 
Q. 
Please describe the physical security counter measures in place for 
1 
KEDNY and KEDLI. 
2 
A. 
There has been a heightened understanding of evolving physical security 
3 
risks over the past several years.  To address this, National Grid 
4 
significantly increased its deployment of physical security assets, such as 
5 
facility access card readers, security cameras, and alarm systems, to meet 
6 
regulatory requirements, enhance customer and employee safety, and 
7 
safeguard assets.  In 2013, National Grid created a centralized 
8 
organization to address physical security issues, the Physical Security 
9 
Control Center (“Security Control Center”).  The Security Control Center 
10 
operates around the clock to monitor physical security incidents and 
11 
provide immediate operations and emergency response for all National 
12 
Grid facilities, including those of KEDNY and KEDLI.  The staff of the 
13 
Security 
Control 
Center 
disseminates 
security 
information 
and 
14 
notifications, monitor cameras, and receives and responds to alarms.  
15 
National Grid has, and is continuing to increase, the number of sites 
16 
monitored by the Security Control Center. 
17 
18 
842

D. 
SAP S/4 HANA Project 
7 
Q. 
What is the SAP S/4 HANA project? 
8 
 A. 
National Grid uses SAP ERP as its back-office platform supporting 
9 
various essential functions, including Finance, Payroll, Human Resources, 
10 
and Supply Chain.  S/4 HANA refers to SAP’s Business Suite 4, which is 
11 
SAP’s next generation platform.   
12 
 
13 
Q. 
How does the S/4 HANA product compare to National Grid’s existing 
14 
SAP platform? 
15 
A. 
National Grid implemented its current version of SAP (SAP ECC 6.0) 
16 
near the end of calendar year 2012.  That implementation was very 
17 
complex and involved replacing disparate legacy systems that had been in 
18 
place over many years for multiple companies.  Although the current 
19 
version of SAP is providing accurate and robust information and services 
20 
845


--- Page 323 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
36 of 55 
 
to meet the organization’s needs, as discussed below, it will no longer be 
1 
supported by SAP.  
2 
 
3 
The new S/4 HANA platform is built on a modern, in-memory database 
4 
and offers personalized user experiences through SAP Fiori.  SAP 
5 
S/4HANA provides a unified financial and management platform to 
6 
centralize process execution, planning, and reporting based on the same 
7 
(single source of the truth) data.  This solution is equipped with a simple 
8 
and intuitive user experience, and the solution offers one common, real-
9 
time view of financial and operational data to help ensure enterprise-wide 
10 
consistency and reduce reconciliation time and errors, thus minimizing 
11 
and/or eliminating the need for human touch-points to enable financial 
12 
process success.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
Why is the S/4 HANA Project proposed now? 
15 
A. 
SAP will no longer support the version of the SAP platform currently 
16 
deployed at National Grid after 2025.  Allowing a critical system such as 
17 
the organization’s back-office financial engine to go out of support carries 
18 
a high degree of risk.  This includes the inability to implement any critical 
19 
security or compliance patches issued by SAP to address any 
20 
vulnerabilities discovered, software incompatibility with future products 
21 
846


--- Page 324 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
37 of 55 
 
and services offered by SAP, inability to scale in an increasing digital 
1 
world to take advantage of leading practices, and overall higher operating 
2 
costs, decreased performance, and reduced overall reliability.  Therefore, 
3 
National Grid is initiating the effort now to determine the best path 
4 
forward to transition onto the next generation SAP platform, S/4 HANA.   
5 
 
6 
Q. 
The 2012 initial SAP implementation did not go smoothly.  What 
7 
confidence does National Grid have that the move to S/4 HANA will 
8 
go well?  
9 
A. 
The 2012 implementation indeed was challenging.  However, National 
10 
Grid has learned much from that implementation to inform the move to 
11 
S/4 HANA.  As an initial matter, the scope of the 2012 implementation 
12 
and the S/4 HANA project are vastly different.  The 2012 implementation 
13 
involved replacing disparate, aged systems with a brand-new system.  
14 
Here, the transition is from an earlier generation of SAP to the latest 
15 
version of the application.  National Grid also is planning for ample 
16 
upfront review and exploration to determine the optimum solution to be 
17 
implemented.  In addition, the IT organizational structure described 
18 
previously, along with the scaled agile delivery model, will provide for 
19 
improved oversight and accountability as well as greater management and 
20 
control of solution deployment risk.   Lastly, as discussed in more detail 
21 
847


--- Page 325 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
38 of 55 
 
below, National Grid is bringing together all change programs such as CIS 
1 
and S/4 HANA into a central transformation team to better implement and 
2 
coordinate these programs.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
What is the anticipated schedule for developing and implementing the 
5 
S/4 HANA project? 
6 
A. 
As mentioned above, support for National Grid’s current SAP application 
7 
terminates at the end of 2025.  National Grid plans to perform discovery 
8 
phase work in FY20.  Such efforts would consider future process 
9 
inventories and mapping those future processes to software solutions to 
10 
limit and rationalize customizations; evaluating onsite vs. cloud hosting 
11 
options; future technology and infrastructure; organizational and human 
12 
resources; and sequencing the scope (processes/systems) into potential 
13 
deployment scenarios.   
14 
 
15 
Following the discovery phase, the project would move into the design 
16 
phase in FY21.  The design phase would refine the accuracy of the 
17 
operations and capital cost developed during the discovery phase, as well 
18 
as the timeline of the project.  This would include developing design 
19 
principles and a detailed Target Operating Model; creating an overall 
20 
release, sprint and test plan; developing a detailed business case, with 
21 
848


--- Page 326 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
39 of 55 
 
benefit profiles, governance and planning; establishing a quality gate 
1 
process; creating a Program Management Office; and designing an 
2 
appropriate change management plan.   
3 
 
4 
The results of the design phase would be used in the realize/build and test 
5 
phase on the project.  The build and test phase is anticipated to be 
6 
structured on the scaled agile model, which will incorporate multiple 
7 
incremental releases (minimum viable products) to reduce risk and 
8 
manage the process.  However, details of how this would be structured 
9 
necessarily must await the results on the preceding project phases.    
10 
    
11 
Q.  
What costs are included for the S/4 HANA project during the period 
12 
covered by this rate filing? 
13 
A. 
National Grid forecasts $34.650 million of operating expense in the Rate 
14 
Year for the design phase of the project that it will be undertaking in 
15 
FY21.  This amount is reflected in Exhibits __ (ITP-7) and __ (ITP-8) and 
16 
contributes to the incremental IT operating expenses allocated to KEDNY 
17 
and KEDLI in the Rate Year.  There are no capital costs included in the 
18 
Companies’ filings for the S/4 HANA project.   
19 
 
20 
849


--- Page 327 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
40 of 55

1 
period?

No.  However, until the design phase is matured, National Grid will not 
3

4 
project cost estimate based on insufficient information, the Companies 
5 
propose to (i) provide the Commission with an update of the costs as part 
6 
of their corrections and updates filing and (ii) defer for future recovery the 
7

8 
costs.

VI. 
Delivering the IT Capital Plan 
10 
Q. 
National Grid is planning to develop and implement a significant 
11 
number of major IT projects over the next few years.  How will 
12 
National Grid ensure its ability to deliver these projects in a timely 
13 
and cost-effective manner? 
14 
A. 
National Grid appreciates that today’s utilities rely increasingly on 
15 
information technology, and the pace of change is unprecedented in both 
16 
the utility industry and in the IT landscape in general.  That is why 
17 
National Grid has made a focused effort to create a robust, well-organized 
18 
IT organization that can meet the challenges facing it.  The structure of the 
19 
IT organization provides the visibility and controls necessary across the 
20 
organization to effectively coordinate and manage multiple resources and 
21 
859


--- Page 337 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
50 of 55 
 
projects.  IT delivery centers are organized such that resources can be 
1 
appropriately rationalized to meet prioritized business needs.  Further, 
2 
National Grid’s supplier relationships enhance resource availability and 
3 
flexibility.   
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please expand upon the steps being taken to ensure the timely and 
6 
cost effective delivery of IT projects. 
7 
A. 
The steps being undertaken to ensure timely and cost effective delivery of 
8 
the IT investments are as follows: 
9 
• IT has modified its organizational structure so that it is tightly aligned 
10 
to the National Grid business units such that the IT Business Partners 
11 
are accountable for the delivery of programs to time and budget. 
12 
• At the same time, the Company has insourced and will continue to 
13 
insource numerous key technology roles including Project Managers 
14 
and Enterprise Architects.  IT is bringing these roles back in-house to 
15 
enhance project delivery capability and modernize the solution 
16 
delivery process.  The Architects will provide simplification and 
17 
standardization across the application portfolio.  The Project Managers 
18 
that are being brought on-board are skilled in modern delivery 
19 
frameworks which will allow projects to be delivered in releases. 
20 
resulting in faster time to market and an increase in productivity and 
21 
860


--- Page 338 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
51 of 55 
 
quality. 
1 
• In addition, the recent re-negotiation of the Application Development 
2 
and Application Maintenance contract resulted in an increase to the 
3 
number of IT solution vendors from two to four; thereby increasing 
4 
National Grid’s overall ability to scale up and respond to the increase 
5 
in business demand. 
6 
• National Grid is bringing together all large change programs such as 
7 
CIS and SAP S/4 HANA into a central transformation team.  This 
8 
consolidation will help ensure that these transformative investments 
9 
are delivered into the business on time and in a manner that maximizes 
10 
value to customers.  The creation of a central team will ensure that the 
11 
multiple change programs are implemented in a coordinated and 
12 
efficient manner across the organization. 
13 
 
14 
For major projects, such as the ones the Panel describes above, National 
15 
Grid also establishes individual project control boards.  These project 
16 
control boards manage project scope and work to assure the availability of 
17 
resources needed to deliver the respective project. 
18 
19 
861


--- Page 339 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
52 of 55 
 
VII. 
IT Operating Costs in the Rate Year and Data Years 
1 
Q. 
What comprises operating costs for the IT function? 
2 
A. 
In simple terms, IT operating costs consist of: (i) initial project expenses 
3 
and post-implementation costs incurred with new projects; and (ii) costs of 
4 
running the projects once they are placed in service.  The first category of 
5 
costs includes O&M incurred to support investment planning during the 
6 
startup stage and post application development costs that may not be 
7 
capitalized under applicable accounting rules.  The second category of 
8 
costs are those costs of running a project once it is in service (“run-the-
9 
business” or “RTB” costs).  These RTB costs include: software licensing 
10 
and maintenance agreements, hardware maintenance agreements, third 
11 
party contracts, consultant/contractor costs, and internal labor.   
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What are the Companies’ projected IT incremental operating costs 
14 
for the Rate Year and the Data Years? 
15 
A. 
KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s projected incremental operating costs for the 
16 
Rate Year and Data Years (excluding the costs of the GBE Program, 
17 
which are presented in the testimony of Company Witness Christopher P. 
18 
Connolly) can be found in Exhibit __ (ITP-8).  These incremental costs 
19 
consist primarily of incremental IT project operating expenses and RTB 
20 
costs for new capital projects (details of which appear in Exhibit __ (ITP-
21 
862


--- Page 340 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
53 of 55 
 
7), incremental operating expenses associated with the CIS Replacement 
1 
program, design phase costs for the S/4 HANA project, and inflation from 
2 
the Historic Test Year.  The projected amounts of these costs for National 
3 
Grid appear on page 1 of Exhibit __ (ITP-8).   
4 
 
5 
These costs are then allocated to KEDNY and KEDLI using the allocators 
6 
identified in Exhibit __ (ITP-8) to arrive at the following incremental IT 
7 
operating expense amounts:

KEDNY:

$18.1 million

VIII. Reporting 
5 
Q. 
What steps do the Companies propose to implement to keep the 
6 
Commission advised of their progress in implementing the IT 
7 
investment plan? 
8 
A. 
As part of the 2017 NMPC Rate Case, Department of Public Service Staff 
9 
proposed that Niagara Mohawk implement periodic reporting of IT project 
10 
development and investment activity in a manner comparable to the 
11 
comprehensive periodic reporting already performed for electric and gas 
12 
capital investment activities.  Niagara Mohawk’s implementation of those 
13 
reporting measures have not only served to better apprise Staff of the 
14 
status of the company’s IT investment projects, it has also brought greater 
15 
internal attention to IT project status and monitoring.  Given the beneficial 
16 
effects such reporting has achieved for Niagara Mohawk, the Companies 
17 
propose to establish IT-related reporting requirements like those adopted 
18 
by the Commission in the 2017 NMPC Rate Case.  Specifically, the 
19 
Companies propose that: 
20 
 
 
(i) 
at the beginning of each Rate Year, the Companies will file 
21 
864


--- Page 342 ---
Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
55 of 55 
 
with the Secretary their IT capital investment plan for that Rate Year, 
1 
including a narrative explanation of the plan and an identification of all 
2 
proposed IT projects and their costs; 
3 
 
 
(ii) 
they will file IT-related quarterly variance reports within 60 
4 
days of the end of each Rate Year quarter that will contain the same 
5 
information as is being provided by Niagara Mohawk; and  
6 
 
 
(iii) 
on a semi-annual basis, the Companies will meet with 
7 
Department of Public Service Staff to discuss the IT investment plan and 
8 
the progress being made on fulfilling the goals of the plan. 
9 
 
 
10 
Throughout this process, the Companies will work in good faith with Staff 
11 
to identify any additional information that would be useful to include in 
12 
the quarterly reports.

II. 
Corrections & Updates 
1 
 
CIS Project 
2 
Q. 
Please describe the corrections and updates associated with the proposed CIS 
3 
project. 
4 
 A. 
There are two changes related to the CIS project: (i) inclusion of inadvertently 
5 
omitted AFUDC; and (ii) an update to the in-service dates initially proposed. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please describe the correction to include AFUDC in the determination of the 
8 
cost of the CIS project. 
 
9 
A. 
Large, longer-term capital projects, such as the planned CIS project, require 
10 
utilities to finance the cost of development prior to the asset going in-service and 
11 
becoming used and useful.  Utilities in New York are compensated for their costs 
12 
to finance such projects through the accrual of AFUDC for recovery in rates.  In 
13 
their initial filings in these proceedings, the Companies inadvertently failed to 
14 
include any AFUDC amount associated with the CIS project in the model used to 
15 
determine the revenue requirements for KEDNY and KEDLI.  The Companies are 
16 
correcting that error by including the appropriate AFUDC amount in their 
17 
corrected and updated revenue requirements.  
18 
 
19 
870


--- Page 348 ---
 
Corrections and Updates Testimony of the Information Technology Panel

2 
$41 million.  Of that amount, $26.7 million is associated with projects that go into 
3 
service during the Rate Years and Data Years, and it is therefore appropriate to 
4 
include KEDNY and KEDLI’s allocable share of this amount in the determination 
5 
of the Companies’ revenue requirements in these proceedings. 
6

11 
from April 1, 2022 to October 1, 2022 for both KEDNY and KEDLI.  See Exhibit

6 
Q. 
Please describe the updates to the capital and operation expense budgets for 
7

8 
Data Years. 
9 
A. 
Each year, National Grid is required to invest significant amounts in IT projects 
10 
needed to implement requirements imposed by governmental authorities.  Such 
11 
authorities include state and federal utility regulators, state or federal lawmakers, 
12 
taxing authorities, and similar bodies.  Based on the experience of being subject to 
13 
annual emergent mandatory IT obligations, National Grid proactively budgets for 
14 
such expenditures and includes an amount in its annual budget for anticipated 
15 
mandatory projects, the details of which are generally not known at the start of the 
16 
budget year.  In their April 30, 2019 filings, the Companies included $20 million 
17 
per year of capital investment for mandated IT projects across National Grid’s US 
18 
operations for the Rate Year and each Data Year as shown in Exhibit __ (ITP-4), 
19 
page 1 of 5, and annual operating expenses of between $1.5 million and $2.5 
20 
million for such projects as shown in Exhibit __ (ITP-7), page 1 of 2.  Subsequent 
21 
872

1

2 
expense budgets and determined that the capital investment amount should be 
3 
reduced to $14 million per year for each of the Rate Year and the Data Years, and 
4 
the operating expense budget should be increased to $6 million per year.   These 
5 
changes are reflected in Exhibit __ (ITP-4CU), page 1 of 5, and Exhibit __ (ITP-

7CU), page 1 of 2.

Updated RTB Costs  
6 
Q. 
Please describe the correction to address omitted RTB costs for projects that 
7 
went or will go into service prior to the end of the Rate Year.   
8 
A. 
RTB costs are the costs of operating and maintaining an IT project once it is in 
9 
service.  Such costs include costs associated with software licensing and 
10 
maintenance agreements, hardware maintenance agreements, and application 
11 
support contracts, as well as consultant/contractor costs, and internal labor.   
12 
 
13 
 
National Grid continually puts new IT projects into service and incurs RTB costs 
14 
associated with such projects.  In developing its operating expense forecast for the 
15 
April 30, 2019 filing, the Companies failed to identify the RTB costs for the 
16 
projects that went in-service, or are planned to go in-service, in the time period 
17 
between the Historic Test Year and the Rate Year.  Additionally, the Companies 
18 
inadvertently omitted RTB costs in Data Years 1, 2, and 3 for certain projects that 
19 
were properly reflected in the forecast for the Rate Year.  The updated RTB costs 
20 
are reflected on Exhibits __ (ITP-7CU) and (ITP-8CU) and total $28.5 million in 
21 
876

Corrections and Updates Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
Page 10 of 10 
the Rate Year, an increase of $1.7 million and $1.0 million for KEDNY and 
1 
KEDLI, respectively, above the level included in the Companies’ April 30 filings.

Q. 
Is National Grid the only utility that has seen a need for increased 
1

2 
A. 
No.  Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc.’s (“ConEd”) most 
3

4

5

6 
spending to meet accelerating business demands.  ConEd’s proposed IT 
7

8

9

11 
 
12 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any specific concerns with the particular regression 
13 
analysis performed by the SITP to forecast IT capital expenditures in the 
14 
Rate Year? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  The linear regression analysis used by the SITP provides no useful 
16 
information concerning what the appropriate level of IT investment should be 
17 
in the Rate Year.  Linear regression is used to determine whether a linear 
18 
relationship exists in a data set such that the relationship can be used to predict 
19 
future results.  The data set is comprised of points that measure the 
20 
relationship between at least two variables:  an independent variable, which is 
21 
891


--- Page 369 ---
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
Page 13 of 28 
expected to be the causative variable, and a dependent variable, which is 
1 
supposed to be the effect.  There is no basis for concluding that a linear 
2 
relationship exists or should exist between fiscal years and IT capital 
3 
investment levels.  Indeed, the Companies’ proposed IT capital investment 
4 
levels fluctuate up and down year over year depending on the nature of 
5 
projected capital investments planned for a given year.  Therefore, a linear 
6 
regression analysis is simply not an appropriate tool to use to forecast future 
7 
investment levels or to develop appropriate levels of IT investment for 
8 
ratemaking purposes. 
9 
 
10 
 
Further, for a regression analysis of past performance to be used to predict 
11 
future results, there should be an expectation that future conditions will be 
12 
similar to the past conditions used for the analysis.  Here, however, the 
13 
regression analysis covers a period that does not include spending such as 
14 
proposed for the CIS or SAP S/4 HANA projects, and therefore is not 
15 
representative of the Company’s future investment expectations.   
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any concerns with the data used by the SITP in 
18 
obtaining its regression result? 
19 
A.

20 
review of the data itself.  While the SITP proposed to analyze the levels of IT 
21 
892


--- Page 370 ---
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
Page 14 of 28 
spending in the period 2015 - 2019, the SITP simply ignored the data for one 
1 
of the five fiscal years, FY 2017, arbitrarily dismissing it as an “outlier.”  At 
2 
the same time, however, as the SITP acknowledges (at 32-33), a significant 
3 
portion of the 2017 IT capital spend characterized as an “outlier” was the 
4 
result of advancing planned investments on projects in the Companies’ 
5 
Technology Improvement Project from “2018 and beyond.”  In other words, a 
6 
significant portion of the FY 2017 actual spend had the effect of suppressing 
7 
actual spending in FY 2018 and beyond; a result that Staff’s regression 
8 
equation makes no adjustment for in 2018 and 2019.

13

14

15

17

18

19

20 
the Companies’ cost projection for this project are very preliminary and 
21 
901

therefore there is a possibility that the actual costs of the project will

3

12

The SITP proposes that (i) IT process improvements implemented for NMPC 
14 
in Case 17-E-0233 et al. be applied to the Companies, and (ii) that Service 
15 
Company should make improvements to its investment request summary 
16 
(“IRS”) and sanction papers.  The Companies generally agree with these 
17 
recommendations as set forth on pages 67-72 of the SITP testimony. 
18 
 
19 
902


--- Page 380 ---
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
Page 24 of 28 
Q. 
What is the Companies’ position regarding the SITP’s recommendation 
1 
to implement specific performance benchmarks related to sanction 
2 
papers?  
3 
A. 
The Companies oppose the Staff’s recommendation regarding developing 
4 
performance indicators relating to sanction papers.  The metrics Staff 
5 
proposes would require substantial resources and time to develop and track.  
6 
Further, although performance benchmarks may be appropriate as part of a 
7 
negotiated resolution of a multi-year rate plan, there is less of a basis for them 
8 
in a one-year litigated case.  Therefore, unless this matter were to proceed to 
9 
negotiation that resulted in a multi-year resolution, the Companies oppose 
10 
establishing performance indicators relating to sanction papers.   
11

Q. 
Please address the SITP’s recommendation that the Companies 
1 
implement enhanced reporting requirements and meet with Staff 
2 
periodically regarding the IT program.   
3 
A. 
The Companies support periodic reporting on IT project status to Staff.  The 
4 
Companies believe greater transparency on IT project selection, prioritization, 
5 
timeline, and budget status will promote increased collaboration between the 
6 
Companies and Staff on the selection and delivery of IT projects and facilitate 
7 
Staff’s review of IT projects as part of rate proceedings for the benefit of New 
8 
York customers.  Indeed, NMPC, KEDNY, and KEDLI provide quarterly 
9 
reporting and meet with Staff on a regular basis regarding their respective 
10 
electric and gas capital investments; and similar to the reporting processes for 
11 
electric and gas assets, NMPC has agreed to provide regular status reports on 
12 
IT projects as part of the Joint Proposal adopted by the Commission in Case 
13 
17-E-0238 et al.  These regular status reports include: 
14 
 
(1) 
an IT Capital Investment Plan for each rate year, including a narrative 
15 
explaining the overall IT investment plan and identifying the proposed 
16 
IT projects and their estimated costs;  
17 
 
(2) 
quarterly variance reports on the status and budgets of major projects, 
18 
details and explanations of budget and timeline variances, reports on 
19 
budget exceptions, and identification of allocations to NMPC; and 
20 
904


--- Page 382 ---
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Information Technology Panel 
Page 26 of 28 
 
(3) 
a year-end report that includes, in addition to quarterly reporting, 
1 
sanction papers for any projects exceeding $1 million that were 
2 
authorized during the previous Rate Year, and reporting on 
3 
performance on any IT projects selected for a cost incentive with Staff. 
4 
 
In addition to these regular status reports, NMPC has agreed to meet with 
5 
Staff on a semi-annual basis to: 
6 
 
 
● discuss budget and actual spending to date, status of in-flight 
7 
projects, proposed projects for the next six months; 
8 
 
 
● review quarterly filings; 
9 
 
 
● develop mutually agreeable sanction paper enhancements needed to 
10 
justify IT investments and allow for adequate review; and 
11 
 
 
● discuss whether different or additional information would be useful 
12 
to include in the quarterly reports. 
13 
 
The Companies would agree to include KEDNY and KEDLI in these 
14 
requirements and processes.

Q. 
Describe the Companies’ Corporate IT budgeting 
18 
process.  
19 
A. 
As stated in the Companies’ response to IR DPS-
20 
432, the Companies state that their Corporate IT 
21 
budgeting process consists of two components: 
22 
the IT Capital Investment Planning Process and 
23 
the O&M Planning Process.  The IT Capital 
24 
955


--- Page 433 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
SITP 
 
 
13 
 
Investment Planning Process starts in May, where 
1 
the budgets are developed, projects and 
2 
prioritized, and estimates refined for the 
3 
upcoming fiscal year, which begins the following 
4 
April.  The O&M Planning Process starts in 
5 
September, where the associated operating 
6 
expenses are developed by using historical 
7 
spending trends and estimating the impact of any 
8 
new projects.  In November, the investment plan 
9 
and O&M budget are implemented in the IT budget 
10 
process and submitted to the global and U.S. 
11 
Chief of Information office for approval.  After 
12 
implementation of the investment, different 
13 
types of monthly meetings are held to discuss 
14 
actual versus budget variances, updates on 
15 
investment spending, and validate or review an 
16 
investment plan that may need to be changed. 
17 
Q. 
Is the Companies’ Corporate IT budgeting process 
18 
appropriate? 
19 
A. 
Yes. 
20 
Q. 
Describe the typical life cycle analysis of an 
21 
IT project and how the individual project 
22 
budgets developed. 
23 
A. 
As shown in the response to IR DPS-1003, the 
24 
956


--- Page 434 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
SITP 
 
 
14 
 
Company explained the five stages of an IT 
1 
project life cycle: pre start-up, start-up, 
2 
requirements and design, development and 
3 
implementation, and close.  The pre start-up 
4 
phase captures the problems and needs to develop 
5 
scope, context, and cost estimates, which is 
6 
shown in the Investment Request Paper (IRS).  
7 
The IRS papers provides a cost estimate of 
8 
project to have a variance of plus 200 percent 
9 
or minus 50 percent.  Next, the project goes 
10 
into the start-up phase, where a project manager 
11 
is assigned to the project to develop a team, 
12 
work plan, and a partial sanction paper.  The 
13 
partial sanction narrows down the variances of 
14 
the projects estimates to plus 25 percent and 
15 
minus 25 percent.  After the partial sanction is 
16 
approved, the project goes into the Development 
17 
and Implementation phase, where the team 
18 
determines the technical requirements needed for 
19 
the project, designs the solution and develops 
20 
the full sanction paper.  The full sanction 
21 
further reduces the variances of the project 
22 
estimates to plus 10 percent to minus 10 
23 
percent.  The sanction paper is submitted to

IT Investment Recovery 
8 
Q. 
How do the Service Companies’ IT capital 
9 
expenditures impact the KEDNY and KEDLI revenue 
10 
requirements? 
11 
A. 
IT project costs are incurred at the Service 
12 
Company level.  The costs are then allocated to 
13 
the individual operating companies that use the 
14 
IT services.  KEDNY and KEDLI thus are allocated 
15 
its proportionate share of IT projects costs for 
16 
each solution it utilized that was developed or 
17 
obtained by the Service Company. 
18 
Q. 
What types of IT costs are allocated to KEDNY 
19 
and KEDLI? 
20 
A. 
The Companies divide their IT program costs into 
21 
three categories: capital expenditures, 
22 
operating expenses, and “run the business,” or 
23 
RTB, expenses. 
24 
964


--- Page 442 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
SITP 
 
 
22 
 
Q. 
Please describe the capital expenditures 
1 
category. 
2 
A. 
Capital expenditures represent the costs to buy 
3 
or create the project that will be included as 
4 
an asset at the Service Company.  
5 
Exhibit___(ITP-4CU), shows the forecast capital 
6 
expenditures, by project, for the Rate Year, as 
7 
well as FYs 2022, 2023, and 2024.   
8 
Q. 
How do these capital expenditures translate to 
9 
KEDNY and KEDLI’s revenue requirements? 
10 
A. 
Once the project is closed to plant in service 
11 
on the Service Company’s books, the Service 
12 
Company begins to charge KEDNY and KEDLI for its 
13 
portion of the amortization expense of the 
14 
projects, as well as a return on the unamortized 
15 
project costs.  This process is similar to that 
16 
used for “traditional” gas plant, whereby the 
17 
Companies incur depreciation expense and also 
18 
earns a return on the net book value when the 
19 
plant is included in rate base. 
20 
Q. 
Do the IT assets move to KEDNY or KEDLI’s books 
21 
after being placed in service? 
22 
A. 
No.  These assets remain on the Service 
23 
Company’s books after KEDNY and KEDLI begin

4 
Maintenance expense, or O&M.  Service Company

14

16 
attack by incorporating redundant security 
17 
mechanisms.  This would require an attacker to 
18 
circumvent each mechanism to gain access to a

21

can affect operations and put disclosure of 
23 
confidential information at risk.  A high 
24

Forecasting Adjustment 
9 
Q. 
What is the Panel’s forecasting adjustment? 
10 
A. 
Our forecasting adjustment reflects a decrease 
11 
to Rate Year capital expenditures based on a 
12 
linear regression of the historic actual 
13 
spending.  
14 
Q. 
What is linear regression? 
15 
A. 
A linear regression is a statistical technique 
16 
used to model the relationship for a set of 
17 
data.  The results of a linear regression 
18 
provide an R-squared value, and two 
19 
coefficients, one being the slope of the line 
20 
and the other being the y-intercept.  Using 
21 
these two coefficients and the equation of a 
22 
line (y=mx+b), it is possible to reasonable 
23 
predict future trends. 
24 
996


--- Page 474 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
SITP 
 
 
54 
 
Q. 
What is the R-squared value and what does it 
1 
represent? 
2 
A. 
The R-squared value is a statistical term that 
3 
indicates how well the line fits to the data, 
4 
which ranges from 0.0 to 1.0, 1.0 being a 
5 
perfect fit.  For any R-squared value below 0.9 
6 
it was determined that the historical data did 
7 
not show a high enough correlation and the 
8 
regression analyses for that group was not 
9 
utilized in our forecast.  
10 
Q. 
Describe how the Panel used linear regression 
11 
analysis to develop its forecasted rate year 
12 
budget. 
13 
A. 
We employed linear regression over FY 2015, 
14 
2016, 2018, and 2019.  We did not use FY 2017 
15 
because we determined the year to be an outlier, 
16 
as discussed earlier in our testimony.   
17 
Q. 
Describe the results of the linear regressions. 
18 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit___(SITP-2), our analysis of 
19 
the historic data showed a good statistical fit, 
20 
an R-squared of 0.932, which shows that the 
21 
historical data we used was a good fit.  We 
22 
found the forecasted Service Company IT capital 
23 
expenditures to be $129.4 million. 
24 
997

Downward Only Reconciliation 
19 
Q. 
Is the Panel concerned that the Service Company 
20 
will under-spend its Rate Year IT budget? 
21 
A. 
Yes.  As previously discussed, the Service 
22 
Company’s historical data shows that there have 
23 
been significant variances between the capital 
24 
1006


--- Page 484 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
SITP 
 
 
64 
 
budget and actual expenditures.  As discussed 
1 
earlier, the Service Company is planning a 
2 
substantial increase in IT spending.  However, 
3 
the Service Company has not provided enough 
4 
support to show that it can ramp-up its spending 
5 
to complete all of the proposed IT investments.  
6 
For these reasons, there is a significant risk 
7 
that the Service Company will again fail to 
8 
execute its spending plan fully and timely, 
9 
thereby forcing KEDNY’s and KEDLI’s customers to 
10 
pay rates based on a level of new plant that is 
11 
not actually deployed. 
12 
Q. 
Does the Panel’s forecasted budgeting adjustment 
13 
address this concern? 
14 
A. 
Partially.  Our forecasted budget adjustment, as 
15 
well as the adjustment to remove CIS, reduces 
16 
the allowed Service Company capital IT spending 
17 
to $129.4 million in the Rate Year.  However, 
18 
despite this reduction to the Companies’ request 
19 
of $243.8 million, it still exceeds the IT 
20 
capital spend in prior years by a significant 
21 
amount.  FY 18 and 19 had IT capital spends of 
22 
$94.6 million and $115.2 million, respectively.  
23 
Although FY 17 reported an IT capital spend of 
24 
1007


--- Page 485 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
SITP 
 
 
65 
 
$122.0 million, we have concerns about the data 
1 
for that period, as we previously discussed. 
2 
Q. 
What do you recommend to address this concern? 
3 
A. 
Similar to what the Commission approved in the 
4 
Niagara Mohawk Rate Order, we recommend a 
5 
Service Company Rents IT and GBE Program Net 
6 
Utility Plant and Depreciation Expense 
7 
Reconciliation Mechanism to protect customers 
8 
from paying delivery rates higher than necessary 
9 
to support the IT assets actually implemented. 
10 
Q. 
Briefly describe the proposed Service Company 
11 
Rents IT and GBE program Net Utility Plant and 
12 
Depreciation Expense Reconciliation Mechanism. 
13 
A. 
Our recommended mechanism would require the 
14 
Companies to reconcile its respective actual IT 
15 
and GBE program average net utility plant and 
16 
depreciation expense revenue requirements to the 
17 
forecast revenue requirements.  The IT and GBE 
18 
program average net utility plant and 
19 
depreciation expense revenue requirement would 
20 
be calculated by applying the Companies’ 
21 
authorized pre-tax weighted average cost of 
22 
capital in the Rate Year to the IT and GBE 
23 
program average net utility plant balance and 
24 
1008


--- Page 486 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
SITP 
 
 
66 
 
adding the depreciation expense to the product.  
1 
At the end of the Rate Year, if the actual IT 
2 
and GBE program average net utility plant and 
3 
depreciation expense revenue requirement is less 
4 
than the target average net utility plant and 
5 
depreciation expense revenue requirement, the 
6 
Companies would defer the revenue requirement 
7 
impact for the benefit of customers. 
8 
Q. 
Should this mechanism be symmetrical? 
9 
A. 
No.  The mechanism should be a one-way, downward 
10 
only true-up.  Therefore, if actual Service 
11 
Company Rent expense exceeds the approved Rate 
12 
Year allowance, a regulatory liability would not 
13 
be established for the Companies to recover from 
14 
customers at a later date.  The calculations for 
15 
the effects of this mechanism for the Rate Year 
16 
should be filed with the Secretary on or before 
17 
July 31st after the close of the Rate Year. 
18 
Q. 
Why does the Panel recommend that the mechanism 
19 
be a one-way, downward-only true-up mechanism? 
20 
A. 
Budgeting and spending are activities wholly 
21 
within the Companies’ control.  Improving their 
22 
performance in these areas also is within the 
23 
Companies’ control.  A symmetrical true-up will 
24 
1009


--- Page 487 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
SITP 
 
 
67 
 
not provide an incentive for the Companies to 
1 
improve their budgeting and spending processes.  
2 
Customers have no control over the Companies’ 
3 
level and pace of spending, yet they bear the 
4 
risk that the Companies’ historic challenges in 
5 
spending to projected levels will continue and 
6 
will be reflected in rates.  The true-up 
7 
mechanism, therefore, should reconcile only 
8 
underspending to allocate these risks equitably 
9 
between the Companies and customers. 
10
